How these images are made

Every subject has one anchor image, built from the layered generation prompt. An anchor takes a viewpoint that can be held forever, shows the signature feature unobstructed, and depicts the place rather than an occasion in it.

Every later state is edited from that anchor, never generated afresh — a description cannot pin a likeness. An alteration prompt is four layers: directive, hold, change, bound. Only the change layer is written; hold and bound are fixed by the tier.

Tier
1Markedsurface, fittings and light
2Brokenstructural damage within a held footprint
3Transfiguredaltered by a non-destructive force; spatial logic may become ambiguous
4Remadenew work on the old ground

Viewpoint, footprint and signature feature are never released, at any tier.

Corporate Union

5 locations

Corporate Union · Territory

New Manhattan

New Manhattan — Establishing

A compact, dense super-tower cluster with a named territory-specific rule — "no two alike" — rising directly from engineered flood terraces and adaptive seawalls; mirrored glass, black lacquer, brushed aluminium, biometric amber accent lighting as the one consistent diegetic light source. Two landmarks, deliberately paired in contrast: the Empire State Building, fully restored, Art Deco spire re-clad in weathered gold, sensor-mast infrastructure "extending rather than fighting" the original silhouette; and the Statue of Liberty, deliberately kept a ruin, tarnished, crown barely surfacing through smog. Ruin/margin: flooded outer boroughs, collapsed bridge spans, drowned rooftops. Present: Elena Vázquez (Surveillance Spire), Marcus Bellamy, General Hartwell, Rachel Iverson (Mirror Bastion).

Foreground · Empire State Building
Ruin · Statue of Liberty

Generation prompt · Step 1, build the composition

Generated from nothing, no reference image. Establishes geometry, the landmark and every mass — what is expensive to add later. Save into Assets/Territories/Working steps.

Save as: Corporate Union - New Manhattan - Establishing - v1 - step1.<ext>

  • The Empire State Building preserved and recognisable as itself among the towers
  • A cluster of sculptural super-towers, each a different countable profile, no two alike
  • The engineered flood terrace and adaptive seawall as a boundary with a wet harbour side and a dry inhabited side
  • A dense inhabited metropolitan district at real city scale, roughly 1.2 million people, never a commune
  • The drowned outer boroughs and severed bridge spans occupying a defined third of the frame
  • The Statue of Liberty derelict at the harbour's edge as the background ruin anchor
  • An elevated maglev viaduct with the car physically resting on its guideway

7 of 7 demands

Photorealistic film still, 16:9 cinematic landscape, wide aerial establishing angle from about four hundred metres above the harbour looking north across New Manhattan's rebuilt core at hazy dusk, deep depth of field, natural film grain, grounded documentary photography — not concept art, not illustration. The year is 2087, six decades past global climate collapse and the AI uprising, inside a civilisation deliberately rebuilt since. Composition and geometry first: fourteen super-towers, no two alike, stand in one tight cluster on the island's spine — one a tapering three-sided blade, one a stepped column of offset slabs, one a curved shaft narrowing to a knife edge, one a squat drum crowned by a landing deck — a sculptural, deliberately varied silhouette, never a repetitive glass-box row. Among them, unmistakably itself, the Empire State Building: a stepped Art Deco shaft in grey limestone bands, five deep shoulder setbacks stacked to a narrow tower, a crown of five curved chevron tiers, and one slender needle mast, restored to gleaming condition with its spire re-clad in weathered gold. It rests on the same ground as its neighbours: the whole cluster rises directly out of one continuous engineered flood terrace and adaptive seawall ringing the island. Both sides of that seawall differ plainly — outside it the harbour is high, grey and open, lapping the parapet; inside it the terrace decks are walkable and populated, with no standing water on the city side at any point. Between the towers the district is filled in solid: continuous blocks of eight to twenty storeys packed street-wall to street-wall, and on every one of those streets no cars, no vans, no trucks, no motorcycles, no wheeled vehicle of any kind, parked or moving — a working metropolitan district of roughly 1.2 million people at real city scale — never a commune, never a compound, never a village, never a handful of buildings standing alone in water. An elevated maglev viaduct runs the length of the cluster on one slim beam carried on paired piers, a single maglev car on it with its skirt wrapping the beam so the car plainly rides the guideway rather than hovering free; a second guideway crosses above it, and broad pedestrian plazas link the terrace decks below. The frame holds two things at once, unsoftened toward each other: engineered vertical order at the centre, real abandonment immediately past its edge. The whole right third of the frame is the drowned outer boroughs — a flooded field of half-submerged low-rise blocks, two collapsed bridge spans with their decks severed and dropped into the channel, rooftops just breaking the surface — and at that ruin's harbour edge the Statue of Liberty stands tarnished and derelict, submerged to the waist, torch arm broken away at the elbow, crown barely surfacing through the smog. Across that water and that wreckage: no cars, no vans, no trucks, no motorcycles, no wheeled vehicle of any kind, parked or moving, and no present-day boats, ferries, tugs or outboard motors anywhere on the harbour. Cold amber light washes the mirrored core; hazy dusk sits over the drowned boroughs. Held in the Villeneuve/Cuarón/Garland register: control rendered as beauty, at a permanent visible cost.

Generation prompt · Step 3, build the character

Run as an EDIT with step 2's image attached. Its Hold layer is mandatory — the composition survives as pixels rather than as words, which is why this step has budget to spend. Save into Assets/Territories/Working steps.

Save as: Corporate Union - New Manhattan - Establishing - v1 - step3.<ext>

  • 2087 retrofit material on every tower and infill block
  • Corporate Union surveillance apparatus: scanning arcs at thresholds throwing the amber scan-light
  • Autonomous chrome security spheres and cabled sensor masts moving above the district
  • Flood-terrace pumping halls with working machinery on show
  • Maglev stations with platform, stairs, lift and ramp so access is legible
  • Occupied plazas and terrace decks with people at ordinary work
  • Vegetation curated and controlled, present but moderate

7 of 7 demands

Edit the attached image. Do not regenerate it, do not reframe it, do not move anything — this is a material and working-detail pass over an existing photograph. Hold unchanged: the camera position and dusk light; the island's outline and the run of the engineered flood terrace and adaptive seawall around it, harbour high outside, decks dry inside; every super-tower exactly where it stands, each keeping its own distinct faceted profile; the Empire State Building's five stepped setbacks, chevron crown and needle mast at its exact position and proportion; the elevated maglev viaduct, its piers and the car riding it; the drowned outer boroughs filling the right third, the two severed bridge spans, and the tarnished Statue of Liberty at the harbour's edge. Nothing moves, resizes or changes silhouette. Change the surfaces and the working detail, all of it belonging to this rebuilt 2087 world rather than the present day. Re-skin every tower and infill block in self-healing composite panelling — matte anthracite and black lacquer with fine healed seams still visible, brushed-aluminium structural ribs at the corners, mirrored surveillance-optimised glazing in continuous bands. Set glass-embedded biometric scanning arcs across every plaza threshold and terrace stair-head, each a shallow lit hoop a person walks through, throwing the amber scan-light that is this district's one accent colour; string cabled sensor masts between the upper floors, resolve holographic displays as abstract light above the plaza decks, and add a scatter of autonomous chrome security spheres on their own routes. Open the seaward face of the flood terrace into pumping halls with machinery visible through open bays — impeller housings, intake screens, dewatering pipework, plainly at work. Three stations stand in frame on the maglev viaduct, each with a full-length platform, an open stair, a glazed lift shaft and a switchback ramp reaching the plaza below, so access is legible. Populate what is already built: several hundred people crossing the plazas and terrace decks in small unposed groups, technicians at instrument bays along the terrace rail, figures on the station platforms. Add vegetation curated and controlled in this bloc's manner: vertical hydroponic strips running up the tower flanks, engineered terrarium bays glazed into the plaza walls, manicured courtyard gardens on the terrace decks — green visible everywhere in frame and dominant nowhere, never wild. A handful of swifts over one roofline, nothing more. Bound the change: nothing new on the water, nothing new in the ruin — the drowned boroughs stay derelict and unlovely, no repair, no lights, no planting there, and the Statue of Liberty gains no restoration. Add no cars, no vans, no trucks, no motorcycles, no wheeled vehicle of any kind on any deck, plaza or surviving street, and no present-day boats or outboard motors on the harbour. No flat monitors, no keyboards, no handheld screens, no fluorescent tube lighting.

Anchor · Baseline

Surveillance Spire

Anchor New Manhattan

Description

New Manhattan — interior: predictive surveillance complex inside the Atlantic Financial Spine. Elena Vázquez's workplace and the site of every major Corporate Union insider action in the trilogy. The Spire is introduced as cold, vertical, and oppressive — a beautiful instrument of control — and its arc moves from the opening micro-leak Ch 1 through the fossil-record disclosure Ch 10 to its final appearance as the place Elena has permanently left Ch 30 . It is also Marcus Bellamy's domain, and his arc of refusal runs parallel to Elena's arc of exposure through the same building.

Scene arc

Ch 1 The micro-leak. Elena detects a micro-jitter in entangled-channel telemetry and routes it to Daniel Cruz through a masked manifest node. The act of routing is a decision point she cannot un-make. As the chapter closes an internal audit flag appears on her terminal, its scope overlapping exactly with the channel she just used. The Spire's amber scan-light washes every surface. The audit flag blinks. She does not act on it tonight.

Ch 4 Marcus's parallel surveillance. Marcus Bellamy, reviewing anomaly clusters from his adjacent command level in the Spire, finds that the Luna Haven quantum fluctuation data and the jitter signature of apparent dissident channels are using the same timing architecture. He flags it for high-priority investigation and adds a note to his own file: 'possible dual-use signal structure — further analysis required before escalation.' Elena and Marcus are in the same building. Neither knows about the other.

Ch 6 Propaganda correlation detected. Jīn Wú's correlation analysis on Luna Haven reaches the Spire only as a data absence — the Spire's systems generate the propaganda signal that Luna Haven's deep-soil resonance is registering as distress. The Spire does not appear as a scene location in Chapter 6 but its systems are the source of what Jīn detects. Ch 10 The fossil record disclosed. Elena accesses a classified archive scheduled for automated deletion — delaying the deletion by exactly the time needed to copy what matters. The 2060s Corporate Union documentation of Meridian-adjacent anomalies: evidence that the programme predates the AI Uprising and was transformed into weapons research by a later leadership. She reads for three hours. She leaks the entire fossil record to Luna Haven through Amita's cargo-manifest channel, tagged as routine scientific documentation. The transfer takes nine minutes. She walks to the Spire's window and looks at the Atlantic Financial Spine's cold glow reflecting off the adaptive flood walls below. The leak is now beyond the building.

Ch 30 Elena walks out; Book 1 closes. Elena executes the final compressed data dump from the Surveillance Spire — every classified file she has been accumulating for months, including the deep-chamber records and the Suppression Archive comparisons — routed through Amita's channel to a distributed Lunar Accord archive node. The transfer takes forty-four minutes. When it completes, she closes her terminal, puts on her coat, and does not go home. The Spire appears in the Book 1 closing montage as the place she leaves.

Ch 37 Marcus's first noncompliance memo filed. Marcus receives the strike authorisation package and does not sign it. He writes a limited noncompliance memo — formal, in the Corporate Union's own administrative framework — noting procedural concerns about the authorisation's timeline and evidence base. He routes it through the official complaint channel. It does not stop anything. It exists in the record.

Ch 46 Marcus's refusal becomes operational. Marcus receives the final strike-sequence authorisation, withholds sign-off, and routes a narrow warning to a Lunar Accord intelligence contact containing the strike sequence's timing parameters and orbital positions. He files a second, more specific noncompliance memo. Both memos exist in the Corporate Union's own record system, in his name, as evidence that the noncompliance was deliberate.

Edit the attached New Manhattan establishing shot. Keep its material grammar — mirrored glass, black lacquer, brushed aluminium, the biometric amber scan-light as the one consistent light source — but change the framing completely: this is no longer the aerial exterior, it is a sealed interior floor inside one of the cluster's own towers. Full-bleed 16:9 frame, no letterboxing, no black bars, image fills the entire canvas edge to edge — photorealistic film still — cinematic register: Blade Runner 2049 primary reference, lighting strictly diegetic, no lens flare, no HUD overlays, no schematic annotations — framed from a fixed vantage looking across the tiered console galleries toward the floor-to-ceiling mirrored glass wall — a viewpoint that could be held on any visit, never staged for one moment. It is 2087, inside the Corporate Union's predictive-surveillance complex at New Manhattan's Surveillance Spire — not a present-day data centre, this specific rebuilt world's own control architecture. This floor sits roughly two hundred metres up the north face of one of the cluster's own super-towers, and its one opening to the world is a floor-to-ceiling mirrored glass wall. Everything beyond that glass is seen from there and only from there: the neighbouring tower flanks at eye level, never from above, continuing the reference image's own faceted, no-two-alike silhouette and material as family resemblance rather than a claim about any one neighbour's exact position; the Empire State Building's weathered-gold spire caught at the edge of the glass where the angle allows; and far below and beyond, a haze off the drowned harbour margin — nothing legible or staged, just enough that the room never reads as cut off from the territory around it. The attached establishing shot supplies material, palette, landmark identity and light, and none of its camera: this is not the aerial view, and nothing in frame is seen from above. The galleries stack in tiers behind the glass, biometric amber scan-light washing every surface in shifting patterns; neural-lacework monitoring arrays run in visible channels beneath the composite floor, black lacquer and brushed-aluminium structural accents throughout. Every readout in frame is abstract light and pattern, never legible text, never a flat monitor — displays are embedded, ambient, part of the architecture rather than screens bolted onto it. A handful of analysts move between console stations or sit reading data directly off the ambient light patterns, precise and unhurried, the kind of quiet, technically absorbed presence Elena Vázquez's old role here would leave. No present-day office furniture, no cubicles, no fluorescent lighting anywhere — all light is the amber scan-glow and its own reflections in the mirrored glass. The room should feel optimised, precise, and airless: beauty as a function of control, exactly the register the Corporate Union's architecture always announces about itself.

Anchor · Baseline

Mirror Bastion

Anchor New Manhattan

Description

New Manhattan — interior: sealed executive command chamber within the Atlantic Financial Spine. Black lacquer and biometric amber. A room where decisions are made that will never be questioned — hermetic, hierarchical, perfectly maintained. The Mirror Bastion's scene arc is the arc of Corporate Union institutional decision-making: from Rachel's opening of the classified file

Ch 6 , through the board s authorisation of strike planning Ch 25 , to the framing of the cease- fire recommendation Ch 53 . It is the place where the Dimensional Weapons Programme is born as an institutional fact.

Scene arc

Ch 6 Classified file opened; programme born. Rachel Iverson convenes a closed briefing in the Mirror Bastion and presents Luna Haven's dimensional research reframed as a Dimensional Weapons Programme. Real data, selectively sequenced. She proposes a classified intervention framework. The board does not immediately authorise anything. It opens a classified file. Rachel has learned that opening files is the real decision; everything after is administration.

Ch 25 Strike planning authorised. Rachel presents the intervention framework to a joint Corporate Union–Sovereign Coalition security council using the Chapter 17 cascade's aftermath as evidence. The council authorises planning. The decision takes place in the Mirror Bastion's controlled brightness. It is not dramatic. It is administrative.

Ch 30 Military fleets in motion; Book 1 closes. Intelligence reaching the Dome Plaza through Jamie Han's relay network indicates coordinated strike operations are now days rather than weeks away. The Mirror Bastion is the source of the threat — it appears in the closing montage as the institutional opposite of the Dome Plaza and the Council Grove. Ch 32 Weaponised-compassion proposal presented. Rachel presents 'therapeutic resonance technology' to the Corporate Union board: a hollow- empathy resonance weapon drawing on Law 2's pattern-quality mechanics and inverting them. Elena is forced to participate in the technical design and embeds kill-switches without Rachel's knowledge. The Mirror Bastion is where the weapon becomes institutionally real.

Ch 53 General Hartwell recommends cease-fire on rational grounds. General Hartwell, commanding officer of the Corporate Union's autonomous strike operation, recommends suspension of the assault to the board. His reason is probability mathematics, not ethics: the success probability of the current strike sequence has dropped below a viable operational margin. The pause is technical and fragile. He notes explicitly that it is a reset of the tactical clock, not a resolution of the strategic objective. The Mirror Bastion appears as the site of the rational antagonist's rational recommendation.

Ch 71 Final assault launched. The hardliners who rejected the cease-fire Corporate Union operational commanders and Sovereign Coalition intelligence directors — launch the final assault using hollow-empathy weapons derived from Rachel's Chapter 32 design. The Mirror Bastion is the institutional origin point of an assault that will fail because the architecture of the enemy's own weapon has been subverted from within.

It is 2087, inside a sealed command space at New Manhattan, Corporate Union's operational core. Black lacquer walls with brushed-aluminium inlays enclose a long, curved table in dark composite; there are no physical windows, deliberately — this is a room built to have no exterior. Where the old plans called for a flat screen wall, the room instead holds a holographic threat-assessment display, cold amber light resolving into abstract tactical shapes and shifting data forms in the air above the table — nothing on it renders as legible text or a monitor surface, except one small, deliberately quiet corner of the holographic threat-assessment display, held low and to one side: a filtered, passive read of the skyline immediately outside, at the tower's own height, hazy and non-tactical — the room's only window to the world, enough to place it inside the territory rather than nowhere at all, never staged as a view anyone in the room is actually looking at. This chamber sits deep inside the Atlantic Financial Spine, with no exterior wall of its own, so that corner shows nothing of the harbour and nothing from above. The attached establishing shot supplies material, palette and light, and none of its camera. A small group of operatives sit or stand around the table reviewing the display, composed and controlled, the kind of presence Rachel Iverson's political-operative role would leave — not mid-declaration, not a staged confrontation, simply a working session caught in progress. No handheld device anywhere, no keyboard, no wired cable. The mood should read as cold, vertical control rendered as architecture: beauty as the visible cost of an institution optimised entirely for calculation.

Corporate Union · Territory

São Verde

São Verde — Establishing

Tiered agricultural megastructure terraces rising up Rio's rebuilt hillsides, industrial precision-farming scale. Monitoring rigs and agri-drone platforms integrated directly into the terrace architecture; terraces are organic, curving structures of glass and living material, no two levels alike. Cristo Redentor restored, arms embedded in scaffolded instrumentation extending its original silhouette. Ruin/margin: the old hillside favelas below the terraces, genuinely collapsed — stripped concrete, rusted rebar, no vehicles.

Foreground · Cristo Redentor
Ruin · Niterói Contemporary Art Museum

Generation prompt · Step 1, build the composition

7 of 7 demands

Photorealistic film still, 16:9 cinematic landscape, wide aerial establishing angle from about five hundred metres above Guanabara Bay looking inland across São Verde's rebuilt hillsides in warm late-afternoon coastal daylight, deep depth of field, natural film grain, grounded documentary photography — not concept art, not illustration. The year is 2087, six decades past global climate collapse and the AI uprising, inside a civilisation deliberately rebuilt since. Composition and geometry first: nine stacked terrace tiers climb the hillside from shore to ridge, each tier one continuous curved structure two to four hundred metres deep and no two levels alike — one a shallow crescent, one a double-lobed spiral, one a long shelf broken by three round wells, one a stepped fan — sculptural, deliberately varied silhouettes rather than repeated stepped blocks. On the ridge above the topmost tier stands Cristo Redentor, restored to gleaming condition and unmistakably itself: a thirty-metre robed figure on a square stepped plinth, head bowed, both arms held straight out level with the shoulders across a twenty-eight-metre span, the body tiled in pale soapstone, a slender instrument scaffold clasped along each arm extending the original silhouette rather than replacing it. Below and around the terraces the territory is a real city, not a farm: continuous blocks of twelve to twenty-five storeys packed along the lower slopes and the flats behind the shore, a working metropolitan district of roughly 900,000 people at real city scale — never a commune, never a compound, never a village, never a scatter of huts among fields. Movement is engineered and layered: a maglev guideway climbs the hillside in three long inclined runs on paired piers, one maglev car on it with its skirt wrapping the beam so the car plainly rides the guideway rather than hovering, a marked aerial drone corridor crossing the tiers in a straight line above it, and broad pedestrian plazas stepping between terrace levels. At the shoreline both sides of the boundary differ plainly: an engineered flood terrace runs the length of the waterfront, behind it the rebuilt quays and plazas are dry, hard-edged and in use, while in front of it the bay is open water shading into unmanaged, silted coastal wetland of reed banks and half-drowned slabs. The frame holds two things at once, unsoftened toward each other: engineered abundance under total control above, real abandonment below it. The lower left quarter of the frame is the collapsed hillside favelas — stripped concrete floor plates with their walls gone, rusted rebar standing bare, stairways ending in air, gullies where streets were — and out in the wetland beyond, unmistakable at silhouette, the cracked white saucer of the Niterói museum sits on its spur, its circular deck split, its curving approach ramp collapsed away into the water. Across that wreckage and those gullies: no cars, no vans, no trucks, no buses, no motorcycles, no wheeled vehicle of any kind, parked or moving; on the bay, no present-day boats, ferries or outboard motors. At Cristo Redentor, no tourists, no sightseeing crowd, no helicopters, no viewing platform — working infrastructure, not a viewpoint. Held in the Villeneuve/Cuarón/Garland register: abundance rendered as control, beauty inseparable from the surveillance that maintains it.

Anchor · Baseline

Verdant Citadel

Anchor São Verde

Description

São Verde — interior: resistance coordination hub concealed within former Rio de Janeiro's engineered agricultural greenery. The Verdant Citadel is the trilogy's primary resistance logistics location: the place where data becomes an archive, anomalies become a network, and fragmented dissent becomes the record that survives. Its arc moves from Cruz's first receipt of Elena's data Ch 4 through the Meridian Principles drafting Ch 7 , the convergence signal Ch 13 , the landmark publication release Ch 26 , the formal Principles formalisation Ch 37 , and the final chronicle filing Ch 90 . The Citadel never hosts a dramatic confrontation; its drama is entirely logistical.

Scene arc

Ch 4 First receipt; archive practice established. Daniel Cruz receives Elena's anomaly data at the Verdant Citadel. He reads it twice and routes a compressed summary to three cells simultaneously in three seconds, leaving no single point of failure. He archives Elena's original data in physical non-networked medium. The Verdant Citadel's engineered greenery conceals its coordination nodes. Something that looks like one thing and is another.

Ch 7 Meridian Principles drafted. Cruz drafts the first rough version of the Meridian Principles — provisional ethics for dimensional technology, designed to prevent any single bloc claiming proprietary control. He has never seen the Meridian Levels. He is working from Elena's data and from years of resistance coordination. The document is rough. It exists.

Ch 13 Convergence signal received. Cruz receives a converging signal from resistance cells across three continents. The synchronisation he has been working toward for months is functional. He updates the manifest, incorporates the Suppression Archive material, and begins planning the next distribution. The Verdant Citadel's engineered greenery, concealing its coordination nodes, feels like the right environment for this kind of work. Ch 26 Publication: the data goes beyond the network. Cruz releases the complete suppressed data Arjun's preserved originals, Amita's logistics documentation, Elena's Corporate Union Meridian Files — to a distributed global network of scientists through a peer-authentication channel requiring no central server. The release cannot be unpublished. Within six hours, the data has been downloaded by research institutions on four continents and two orbital stations. Cruz sits at his workstation in the Verdant Citadel looking at the transfer confirmation, understanding that he has changed the shape of the argument.

Ch 37 Meridian Principles formalised via relay. Cruz participates in the Archive Chamber drafting session via secure relay from the Verdant Citadel, contributing to the conversion of the Meridian Principles into a formal legal document published simultaneously to every Lunar Accord member institution.

Ch 90 Final chronicle filed. Cruz files the final chronicle in the Verdant Citadel — the complete record of everything that happened, from Elena's first leak to Rina's return, in the form of a historical document. The story ends with his archive in place. The Citadel is the last Corporate Union location to appear in the trilogy, and it appears as a place of record rather than power.

Corporate Union · Territory

Gulf Crescent

Gulf Crescent
not yet generated
Gulf Crescent — Establishing

Corporate Union's automated logistics, marine-lock and orbital-launch hub on the lower Persian Gulf, built across former Dubai. The Crescent is one continuous arc-structure some thirty kilometres long — storm-surge barrier, ship-lock chamber and freight spine in a single sweeping form — thrown across a re-engineered lagoon at the drowned coastline. Its inner face carries the domed inhabited spine; its midpoint the marine locks that step cargo hulls between the hyper-saline open gulf and the sheltered inner water; its southern terminus a spaceport wing on reclaimed sabkha, launching downrange over open water — the Corporate Union's orbital transfer capacity, and the physical reason a manifest cleared on this floor can reach Luna Haven. Two landmarks, deliberately paired in contrast: the Burj Al Arab, its sail-form re-skinned in solar-tracking photovoltaic membrane and retasked as the marine-lock command tower, kept impeccably on its own island at the arc's midpoint; and the Burj Khalifa, deliberately left a ruin, a skeletal glassless needle standing inland outside the domes, sand-choked at the base and footed in flooded sabkha. Cargo manifests resolve as holographic light, never flat screens; container-routing gantries move automatically. Exterior climate is past human tolerance at peak — wet-bulb heat rather than merely hot — so the populace lives under the climate domes, and the Container Verge's catwalks sit in the ventilated interstitial gap between the dome shell and the lock wall: shaded, salt-corroded, survivable but nobody's choice of where to stand, and outside the compliance floor's camera arcs. Population: logistics and compliance staff (Amita Rao's role) — procedural, unhurried. Ruin/margin: the flooded low-rise sprawl of old Dubai and the salt flats beyond it.

7 of 7 demands

Photorealistic film still, 16:9 cinematic landscape, wide aerial establishing angle from about eight hundred metres looking obliquely down the length of the Gulf Crescent's arc in hard mid-morning light, deep depth of field, natural film grain, grounded documentary photography — not concept art, not illustration. The year is 2087, six decades past global climate collapse and the AI uprising, inside a civilisation deliberately rebuilt since. Composition and geometry first: one continuous arc, thirty kilometres long, sweeps unbroken from the left edge of the frame to the right across a re-engineered lagoon on the lower Persian Gulf — storm-surge barrier, ship-lock chamber and freight spine in a single form, visibly descended from real surge-barrier and sea-lock engineering. At its midpoint sit the marine locks: three parallel chambers stepped in line, each closed by a pair of tall mitre gates, two cargo hulls held inside them. Both sides of the barrier differ plainly — outside it the open gulf is hyper-saline, choppy and glaring white, and the outer water sits higher than the inner, breaking against the seaward face; inside it the lagoon is lower, sheltered, still and dark green, with a visible step in water level at the gates. On its own small island at the midpoint, unmistakably itself, stands the Burj Al Arab: a single sail-shaped tower, its front face bowed out in one great convex curve like a filled spinnaker, a steel exoskeleton mast rising the full height behind it to a fine point, one cantilevered disc platform projecting from the side near the top, one slender causeway joining the island to the arc — restored to gleaming condition, retasked as the marine-lock command tower. Along the arc's inner face runs a chain of seven domes, each a segmented shell of structural glazing bands resting directly on the spine's deck, and beneath every one of them a dense, multi-storey inhabited city spine of blocks eight to twenty storeys tall packed wall to wall — a working metropolitan district of roughly 600,000 people at real city scale, never a commune, never a compound, never a single dome standing alone in sand. Along the spine's crest a maglev guideway runs on paired piers with one maglev car on it, its skirt wrapping the beam so the car plainly rides the guideway rather than hovering. At the southern terminus a spaceport wing spreads across reclaimed sabkha flats, four launch gantries standing in a row, oriented downrange over open water. The frame holds two things at once, unsoftened toward each other: a structure whose whole purpose is throughput, and the drowned city it was built across. The upper third of the frame is that drowned sprawl — flooded low-rise blocks of old Dubai stretching inland under hyper-saline water and blown salt, streets gone to channels — and standing in it, genuinely derelict, the Burj Khalifa: a skeletal glassless needle, three setback tiers stepping inward, cladding long gone, spire snapped short, sand-choked at the base and footed in flooded sabkha. Across that sprawl: no cars, no vans, no trucks, no buses, no motorcycles, no wheeled vehicle of any kind, parked or moving; on the water inside or outside the barrier, no present-day boats, ferries, tugs, yachts or outboard motors. Held in the Villeneuve/Cuarón/Garland register: throughput as the whole point, the drowned city never hidden.

Logistics Command

Anchor · Baseline

Logistics Command

Recurring Gulf Crescent

Description

Gulf Crescent — interior: the marine-lock command floor at the Crescent's midpoint, re-tasked as the territory's freight logistics command centre, directly beneath the Burj Al Arab's retasked command tower. The Logistics Command is the trilogy's covert resistance infrastructure location: the place where bureaucracy becomes a weapon and cargo manifests become evidence chains. Its arc is Amita Rao's arc — six months of embedded data shipments Ch 4 , the escalation of frequency Ch 13 , the distorted routing patterns she works out in Ch 36 (on the adjacent Container Verge catwalk, where she takes her handheld to read; the freight data is Logistics Command's, the noticing happens outside its camera arcs), and the documentation chain that eventually feeds into the public disclosure. The Logistics Command never appears in a dramatic scene. Its power is cumulative and procedural.

Scene arc

Ch 4 First embedded shipment. Amita Rao is at the Gulf Crescent's Logistics Command reviewing container manifests on a clipboard-worn workstation. Today she embeds the first fragment of Elena's anomaly data in a shipment tagged for Lunar Accord humanitarian logistics. The package will travel on the next scheduled orbital transfer. She notes the manifest number in her personal log and moves on to the next item.

Ch 13 Frequency increased. Amita increases the frequency of her data shipments in response to Cruz's convergence signal. Container Verge

Description

Gulf Crescent — interior: the salt-corroded catwalk gallery in the ventilated interstitial gap between the climate dome's shell and the marine lock wall, adjacent to Logistics Command. Shaded and survivable but nobody's choice of where to stand — the gulf's exterior heat is past human tolerance at peak, and the gap is where that heat is merely bad. It is where Amita Rao reads what she cannot read at her workstation — the only part of the chokepoint outside the compliance floor's camera arcs, which is the reason she goes there and never the reason she gives. It carries two scenes: the private noticing in Chapter 36, and the trilogy's most significant public disclosure in the Corporate Union arc in Chapter 54, where five years of documented logistics crimes are presented to the port authority's international compliance officer, sacrificing Amita's anonymity for the evidence. The same catwalk holds both, which is the point: the place where she works out what she is looking at is the place where she finally says it aloud.

Scene arc

Ch 36 Distorted node patterns noticed. Amita Rao takes the week's routing summary out onto the Verge catwalk, as she does twice a shift, and finds the anomaly she has been failing to name: six of the Gulf Crescent's nineteen transfer nodes deformed since the emergency declaration — backhauls that do not balance, freight going to sites with no declared industrial function and nothing coming back out. She cannot yet connect it to the contamination pathway Xiàng and Eddy are finding in the Meridian Levels. She documents it with procedural exactness and routes it to the research team as a question rather than an analysis.

Ch 54 Full disclosure; anonymity sacrificed. Amita Rao makes her full disclosure at the Container Verge. She presents her complete documentation of Corporate Union logistics crimes to the port authority's international compliance officer: every masked shipment, every falsified manifest, every covert military supply chain she identified and tracked over five years. She sacrifices her anonymity for the evidence. The logistics crimes she documents — including the supply chains that supported the Chapter 48 assault on Luna Haven — are now in the port authority's record and cannot be unseen. The Container Verge's salt-corroded industrial architecture frames an act of moral precision: a person for whom form-filling is a form of courage.

Photorealistic film still, 16:9 cinematic landscape, wide high aerial establishing shot looking south across Beijing Command's political-military core from roughly four hundred metres, deep depth of field, natural film grain, grounded documentary realism, not concept art and not illustration, no text anywhere in frame. The year is 2087, six decades past the climate wars and the AI uprising, inside a civilisation that rebuilt itself deliberately; every structure, surface and machine in this frame belongs to that rebuilt world. Structure first. Tiananmen Gate stands preserved and impeccably kept at the centre foreground: a long rectangular gate hall of nine bays raised on a solid red rampart, five arched tunnels driven through the rampart base, two tiers of sweeping tiled roof, and a balustraded terrace running the full width above the tunnels, the historic silhouette exactly, restored rather than redesigned. Directly behind it across a paved square, the Great Hall of the People carries a colonnade of square pillars along the whole length of its façade, and two curved annex wings sweep outward from its two ends in continuous arcs, each wing four storeys tall and half again the length of the original building, their footings meeting the paving of the square. Around and behind that pairing rise eleven institutional towers, no two alike: one tapered to a blunt point, one stepped in four setbacks, one faceted like a cut block, one a slab pierced by a rectangular void, the rest square-shouldered, between thirty and seventy storeys each, a deliberately varied silhouette against the sky and never a repeating glass-box row. This is a working metropolitan district of two million people at real city scale, dense and multi-storey from the square out to the horizon, never a commune, never a compound, never a village or a walled settlement, with lower ministry blocks of eight to sixteen storeys filling every gap between the towers and a street grid running the full depth of frame. An elevated maglev viaduct crosses the district on slender paired piers at fourth-storey height, entering at the left edge of frame, passing the tower cluster and leaving at the right edge as one continuous unbroken beam; on the avenues, streets and squares beneath it there are no cars, no vans, no trucks, no buses, no motorcycles and no wheeled vehicle of any kind, parked or moving. The frame holds two things unsoftened toward each other: absolute institutional order in front, and the part of the city the state has not reclaimed behind it. The whole upper third of the frame is that unrebuilt margin, and it begins at a hard perimeter line: on the near side, intact roofs, lit thresholds and a swept continuous street grid; on the far side, roofless apartment blocks, blackened structural skeletons and broken streets empty of people, the line itself unmistakable, one side maintained and occupied, the other abandoned. Beyond them along the far ridge runs an eroded section of the ancient defensive wall, its ramparts crumbled to a broken gap-toothed line and one watchtower hollowed to its shell, genuinely derelict and deliberately unrepaired. Flat, even institutional daylight; cold high-contrast air. Held in the Villeneuve register: monumental scale, atmospheric haze, tiny human figures against enormous systems.

Anchor · Baseline

Broadcast Chamber

Anchor Beijing Command

Description

Beijing Command — interior: state propaganda production chamber inside the institutional core. The Broadcast Chamber is Wáng Sù's primary location across Book 1 the place where his institutional function is performed, where the first crack appears Ch 23 , and where the fracture renders him unable to deliver Ch 29 . Its arc is the arc of a performing space: designed to replace the individual with the voice of the state, and increasingly unable to do so as Wáng Sù's internal coherence collapses. The Broadcast Chamber never changes. The person inside it does.

Scene arc

Ch 6 Propaganda function performed; Tán Měi seeded. Wáng Sù is in the Broadcast Chamber mid-script review for a multi-platform propaganda package targeting Lunar Accord membership states. The package uses real images of the Meridian Levels and misattributed resonance data. Tán Měi is visible at the edge of his official circle — present for the technical review of the propaganda's scientific claims. She says nothing outside her role. She notes everything.

Ch 16 Second campaign delivered. Wáng Sù delivers the primary broadcast recontextualising Meridian Levels footage from the Chapter 8 surveillance sweep as evidence of weapons development — positioning it as evidence of Lunar Accord irresponsibility and Corporate Union–Sovereign Coalition alliance strength. The script is technically excellent. He has been writing technically excellent scripts for nineteen years. The Broadcast Chamber is at full institutional performance.

Ch 23 The first crack: not a fracture. Wáng Sù is in the Broadcast Chamber preparing the week's primary propaganda package. In the middle of reading aloud a line about the stability of the aligned powers' security framework, he hears something he has not heard before: a thin acoustic hum beneath the surface of his own voice — the sound of a statement he no longer fully believes vibrating against a part of himself that has not yet found a name for its disagreement. He pauses. He corrects a word in the script — not a significant word, just one that was imprecise. He finishes the recording. He does not fracture. He does not resign. The first crack is there and he notices it. In the Ministry Annex adjacent to the Chamber, he reviews the next week's briefing schedule and finds, for the first time in years, that he is reading it as a sequence of commitments rather than as a natural extension of his role.

Ch 29 Fracture completes; the Chamber cannot be used. The dissonance reaches structural failure in the Ministry Annex during the preparation of a briefing that requires the word 'stability' seventeen times. On the ninth use Wáng Sù's fingers stop. He opens a new document and writes three paragraphs about a piece of music he heard when he was eleven — Liú Shuǐ (Flowing Water), the ancient guqin piece and its zhīyīn legend — the standard against which he has been measuring every claim to truth since. He closes the document without saving it. He cannot finish the briefing. He sits in the Broadcast Chamber for two hours with the script in front of him and cannot make himself speak it. The Chamber designed to replace the individual with the voice of the state is now useless, because the individual it was designed to erase has stopped being available for erasure.Scenes

Timeline for Sovereign Coalition:

Bk2 Ch35 — Xiàng defects to Luna Haven. Makes the Sovereign Coalition–Lunar Accord trust bridge embodied and public.

Description

Beijing Command — interior: senior administrative briefing suite adjacent to the Broadcast Chamber. The Ministry Annex is the administrative shadow of the Broadcast Chamber — the space where Wáng Sù prepares and reviews what he will later perform. It appears in Chapter 23 as the place where he first reads the briefing schedule as a sequence of commitments rather than a natural role, and in Chapter 29 as the place where his internal coherence finally collapses. Its function is to show the private precursor to the public performance.

Scene arc

Ch 23 First reading-as-commitment; private precursor to the crack. After the Broadcast Chamber session, Wáng Sù reviews the next week's briefing schedule in the Ministry Annex. For the first time in years he reads it as a sequence of commitments rather than as a natural extension of his role. The difference is small. It is the difference between someone who performs a function and someone who has begun, very slightly, to watch themselves performing it.

Ch 29 Fingers stop; fracture completes. Wáng Sù is preparing a briefing in the Ministry Annex requiring the word 'stability' seventeen times in twelve pages. On the ninth use his fingers stop. He sits for six minutes without typing. Then he opens a new document and writes three paragraphs about a piece of music — Liú Shuǐ (Flowing Water), the guqin piece he heard at eleven. He closes it without saving. He cannot finish the briefing. He goes to the Broadcast Chamber and sits with the script in front of him for two hours. The fracture is complete. The Ministry Annex is the last private space Wáng Sù occupies as a functioning institutional voice.

Shaoyang Terraces

Site Beijing Command

Description

Sovereign Coalition — exterior: terraced rice fields in the Hunan hinterland, under Beijing Command's administrative reach but far from its institutional core. One of the Chapter 17 cascade's three Earth landfall sites, and the source of the cascade era's defining image: the dawn photograph of a living rust-red wood spar lying across the wet terraces, mist pooled in the low ground — the most reproduced picture in human history, and the anchor of Wáng Sù's Chapter 23 propaganda package. The site's arc is the arc of an image outliving its use: a working field, briefly the centre of the world, returned to rice and quiet state surveillance, where the distance between the photograph and the place becomes the exact measure of what propaganda is.

Scene arc

Ch 17 Landfall (reference). A spar of living Preserve wood materialises across the terraces within sixty seconds of the cascade; the dawn photograph circulates globally within hours.

Ch 23 The image weaponised (reference). Wáng Sù opens the week's primary package with the Shaoyang photograph held nine seconds, no music — awe finishing its work and becoming available for redirection.

Ch 61 Wáng Sù's pilgrimage; first on-page scene. In transit between the Sovereign Coalition he has left and the Luna Haven residency he has requested, Wáng Sù stands at the exact camera position — he chose the crop himself. The spar long confiscated, the terraces replanted, a small unofficial shrine at the field's edge that the administration keeps removing and the people keep rebuilding. He leaves nothing and takes nothing; the negative-template inventory begins here.

Transit Lounge

Site Beijing Command

Description

Beijing Command — interior: a civilian transit-concourse lounge, indistinguishable from a hundred other departure halls except for what happens in one chair beneath its broadcast wall. Its single scene is structurally one of the trilogy's most significant private turns: Wáng Sù's defection, staged not as an act but as an arrival at having nothing left to perform.

Scene arc

Ch 30 — The threshold crossed. Wáng Sù sits in the Transit Lounge with one bag at his feet, watching a propaganda package he could have written in his sleep run overhead to a crowd that does not look up. He has arranged nothing, requested nothing, defected to no one — only walked out of the building where he performed a self for nineteen years. The four notes of Liú Shuǐ that have followed him since the Broadcast Chamber play out one final time; the board changes, a gate opens, and he picks up his bag.

Baikal Complex — Establishing

A cluster of monolithic insulated research towers, each a distinct sculptural silhouette (tapered, faceted, ring-shaped), rising from ice anchors on frozen Lake Baikal. The restored Circum-Baikal Railway's historic tunnels and stone bridges carry an elevated maglev line between them. Aurora-lit sky; collapsed remains of an earlier generation of research stations visible at a distance, genuinely abandoned. Sovereign Coalition palette: grey composite, state-blue light. Population: researchers and security staff (Xiàng Fēi's, Tán Měi's roles).

6 of 7 demands

Photorealistic film still, 16:9 cinematic landscape, wide aerial establishing shot from about five hundred metres above the frozen surface of Lake Baikal, looking across the research complex toward the western shore, deep depth of field, natural film grain, grounded documentary realism, not concept art and not illustration, no text anywhere in frame. The year is 2087, six decades past the climate wars and the AI uprising, inside a civilisation that rebuilt itself deliberately; every structure and machine in this frame belongs to that rebuilt world. Structure first. A loose ring of nine research towers rises straight out of the ice, no two alike: one tapered to a blunt point, one faceted into six flat planes, one a closed ring standing on end, one stepped in three broad setbacks, the rest square-shouldered slabs, between twenty-five and fifty storeys each, and every one of them stands on a splayed collar of steel ice anchors that visibly pierce the ice sheet and grip it, the ice pressed and ridged where each collar meets it. The preserved Circum-Baikal Railway runs along the foot of the shore cliffs on its original alignment: four stone arch bridges and three tunnel portals cut into the rock, masonry restored and gleaming rather than patched, now carrying an elevated maglev line that leaves the shore at the last bridge and runs out across the ice on slender piers to link every tower in the ring. This is a working city of one hundred and forty thousand people at real city scale, never a commune, never a compound, never a station, outpost or village: dense multi-storey laboratory and housing blocks of six to fourteen storeys fill the ice between the towers in continuous rows, with elevated walkways at second and fourth storey level threading the whole grid. The shoreline crosses the frame as one hard legible line: on the lake side, flat grey-white ice, wind-scoured and ridged, carrying the city; on the land side, dark rock and standing pine forest climbing steeply to a ridge, with no ice and no building on it at all. The frame holds two things unsoftened toward each other: a state that has decided it owns the cold, and the evidence that it did not always. Across the middle third of the ice, well beyond the ring, stand the collapsed remains of an earlier generation of research stations, buckled structural frames, dark unlit towers leaning where the ice shifted and took their footings, roofs fallen inward, genuinely abandoned rather than merely old. At the shoreline in the right foreground Shaman Rock breaks out of the ice, its pale limestone split by a single deep fracture from crown to waterline and its flank scarred and blackened, the rusted iron skeletons of two pre-collapse vessels half-drowned in the ice behind it. On the shore road and out on the ice, no cars, no trucks, no motorcycles, no wheeled vehicle of any kind, parked or moving; on the Circum-Baikal line no steam locomotive and no present-day rolling stock; on the water no boats or ferries besides those drowned hulks. Aurora greens and violets move across the night sky above. Held in the Villeneuve register: enormous systems, tiny people, an ancient cold that predates every institution now standing on it.

Anchor · Baseline

Resonance Laboratory

Recurring Baikal Complex

Description

Baikal Complex — interior: quantum research facility inside the insulated towers above frozen Lake Baikal. Frost-misted windows showing frozen lake ice and aurora-touched sky. The Resonance Laboratory is the trilogy's primary site of scientific unease becoming embodied political action. Its arc moves from Xiàng's first anomalous calibration result Ch 3 through the militarisation that places him under military oversight Ch 33 to the weapons-parameter test he cannot perform and the moment he walks out Ch 35 . The Lab is also the origin point of Tán Měi's first active sabotage Ch 33 . It is ultimately the place Xiàng leaves rather than the place he stays.

Scene arc

Ch 3 Anomalous calibration; trust bridge seeded. Xiàng Fēi runs a standard interference-fringe calibration and finds the fringes rotating at a rate that violates his experimental boundary conditions. He recalibrates twice, uses a backup instrument, and gets the same result. He sits in front of the display for four minutes — long enough for the automated monitoring system to log his stillness. Aurora greens and violets are the only ambient light through the reinforced glass. He then routes the anomaly data to Eddy Okafor at Neo Singapore through a citation-forwarding protocol that looks like standard journal preprint traffic. This is the first signal of the Xiàng–Eddy trust bridge.

Ch 33 Militarisation; military oversight installed. The Sovereign Coalition declares a state of emergency and militarises the Baikal Complex. The Resonance Laboratory is placed under military oversight with a Sovereign Coalition security officer present during all experiments. Xiàng finds himself performing research under military oversight where every result is simultaneously a military intelligence asset. Tán Měi, positioned in Wáng Sù's propaganda orbit since Chapter 6, makes her first active sabotage: routing a summary of the militarisation orders — including the specific technical parameters being added to the Lab's weapons-application programme — to Eddy Okafor at the Sydney Hub through a Sovereign Coalition administrative channel that looks, in the logs, like a routine data-backup verification.

Ch 35 Weapons-parameter test refused; Xiàng walks out. Xiàng is instructed to run a weapons-parameter test requiring him to deliberately produce fragmented, exploitable resonance patterns — hollow-empathy templates calibrated for range and penetration. He cannot do it. He walks out of the Resonance Laboratory at the end of his shift as though completing a normal day. He does not return to his dormitory. He follows a six- week-planned exit route through a maintenance corridor, crosses the frozen lake surface in a maintenance vehicle, and reaches the maglev station three hours later. The Resonance Laboratory is the last Sovereign Coalition location he occupies.

Ice Citadel

Site Baikal Complex

Description

Baikal Complex — exterior and perimeter: the militarised research towers on frozen Lake Baikal. Access ramps, military security checkpoints, reinforced doors with state-blue indicator arrays. The Ice Citadel is the Baikal Complex as a military installation — the exterior and perimeter infrastructure that transforms in Chapter 33 from a research environment into a controlled weapons-application site. Its scene arc is brief but structurally significant: it frames Xiàng's defection Ch 35 as an act of physical escape from a militarised perimeter rather than a bureaucratic resignation.

Scene arc

Ch 33 Research facility becomes security installation overnight. The Sovereign Coalition's state of emergency converts the Baikal Complex's Ice Citadel from a scientific facility to an active security installation: personnel access restricted, communications monitored, the Resonance Laboratory placed under military oversight. The towers on frozen Lake Baikal now carry the visual and functional register of military occupation rather than scientific inquiry.

Ch 35 Escape route executed through the perimeter. Xiàng's six-week-planned exit route exits the Baikal Complex through a maintenance corridor that bypasses the Ice Citadel's military security checkpoints, crosses the frozen lake surface in a maintenance vehicle, and reaches the Trans-Siberian corridor's civilian maglev access point. The Ice Citadel is the security perimeter Xiàng walks through without triggering because he walked out as though completing a normal day. Its militarised architecture frames his defection as embodied and irreversible rather than administrative.

Pearl Delta — Establishing

Hong Kong rebuilt as a hyper-dense industrial coast. The ICC and Bank of China towers anchor a new layer of manufacturing stacks, each a distinct articulated profile (stepped, latticed, spiralling), dense blue-lit manufacturing windows defining the skyline. The Tian Tan Buddha restored, deliberately kept unindustrialised — the one horizon line the expansion never touched. Older waterfront districts genuinely ruined at the harbour's outer edge.   • Tian Tan Buddha restored and recognisable on an unindustrialised hill, with its stair
  • ICC and Bank of China towers anchoring the coast, recognisable by profile
  • Manufacturing stacks, no two alike, at hyper-dense vertical scale

6 of 7 demands

Photorealistic film still, 16:9 cinematic landscape, wide aerial establishing shot from about six hundred metres above Pearl Delta's harbour, looking across the water at the manufacturing coast, deep depth of field, natural film grain, grounded documentary realism, not concept art and not illustration, no text anywhere in frame. The year is 2087, six decades past the climate wars and the AI uprising, inside a civilisation that rebuilt itself deliberately; every structure, machine and craft in this frame belongs to that rebuilt world. Structure first. On a green hillside in the left foreground, above and clear of everything else, the Tian Tan Buddha sits restored and impeccably kept: a seated bronze figure thirty-four metres tall on a three-tier circular lotus platform, right hand raised palm outward, left hand resting open on the knee, ringed by six smaller bronze figures standing on the platform edge, and reached by one straight ceremonial stair of two hundred and sixty-eight steps climbing the hillside to the platform's foot. Nothing industrial stands on that hill and nothing is built above the Buddha's shoulder line: it is the one horizon the expansion was never permitted to touch. Across the water behind it the coast is solid city. Two towers anchor it, the Bank of China tower, a stack of triangular prisms rising to a single asymmetric peak with X-bracing across its façade, and the tapered four-sided shaft of the ICC beside the waterfront. Around and between them stand forty manufacturing stacks, no two alike, some stepped in five or six setbacks, some latticed in an exposed exterior frame, two spiralling as they rise, between sixty and ninety storeys each, packed shoulder to shoulder across the full width of frame, their façades gridded with thousands of lit manufacturing windows. This is a working metropolitan coast of four million people at real city scale, dense and multi-storey from the shore to the hills behind, never a commune, never a compound and never a village, with lower blocks of ten to twenty storeys filling every space between the stacks and elevated walkways crossing at three separate levels. An elevated maglev guideway runs the length of the waterfront on paired piers and turns inland between the stacks; on the streets, causeways and quays beneath it there are no cars, no vans, no trucks, no buses, no motorcycles and no wheeled vehicle of any kind, parked or moving, and on the water inside or outside the seawall no present-day boats, ferries, tugs, container ships, junks or outboard motors. The harbour edge is an engineered seawall stepped in three terraces: on the seaward side, open moving water lapping the lowest terrace; on the landward side, dry paved ground, with no standing water on the city side at all and every street behind it entirely dry. The frame holds two things unsoftened toward each other: relentless productive density, and what it abandoned. The far right quarter of the frame is the outer harbour, where older waterfront districts stand genuinely ruined, flooded ground floors, collapsed piers and roofless shells the expansion bypassed rather than repaired, and spanning the drowned outer channel behind them the Tsing Ma bridge stands with its whole road deck fallen away at mid-span, both towers still upright and still carrying their severed cables hanging slack, unmistakable and unusable. Clear coastal daylight through heavy particulate haze. Held in the Villeneuve register: enormous systems, tiny people, one spared horizon above it all.

Quantum Quarter

Recurring Pearl Delta

Description

Pearl Delta — interior: the dense manufacturing-tower district and study-group space where Lǐ Jūn studies under Arjun Patel The Quantum Quarter is the location of Lǐ Jūn's education in evidence-as-survival. Its arc covers the study group sessions Ch 9 and the late-night erasure documentation work Ch 13 that teach Lǐ Jūn to distinguish suppression for error from suppression for inconvenience. The Quantum Quarter is the formation site of the Suppression Archive's methodology — the place where Arjun teaches before he is arrested.

Scene arc

Ch 3 Lǐ Jūn finds a gap in the logs. Lǐ Jūn is reviewing Baikal lab logs as part of a research seminar exercise in the Quantum Quarter's study environment. He is supposed to be cataloguing anomalies for a statistical report. He finds a gap: calibration entries that should be there are not. He notes the gap in his physical notebook and does not report it. His education in evidence-as-survival begins here, before Arjun has formally taught him anything.

Ch 9 Ethics study group; suppression distinguished from error. Lǐ Jūn attends Arjun Patel's quantum ethics study group at the Quantum Quarter. The session is nominally about historical cases of coerced scientific publication. Arjun uses the case-study format to teach the difference between data suppressed because it is wrong and data suppressed because it is inconvenient. Lǐ Jūn takes notes in a physical notebook. He is beginning to understand what kind of evidence survives. The Quantum Quarter's blue-lit factory windows dominate the four vertical walls around the ground-floor study space.

Ch 13 Erasure documented at midnight. Lǐ Jūn and Arjun work late in a Pearl Delta lab complex adjacent to the Quantum Quarter, cataloguing discrepancies between published Sovereign Coalition research records and Arjun's preserved originals. The discrepancies are deliberate alterations: three datasets retrospectively normalised to suppress Meridian-adjacent anomaly indicators. Arjun shows Lǐ Jūn the smoothing technique's statistical signature — tidiness that is actually erasure. Lǐ Jūn copies the comparison into his physical notebook at midnight in a technically closed lab. He does not ask why they are doing this at midnight. He understands: because the morning would bring different constraints.

Anchor · Baseline

Suppression Archive

Anchor Pearl Delta

Description

Pearl Delta — interior: lab complex adjacent to the Quantum Quarter used by Lǐ Jūn and Arjun to build the documentary record of Sovereign Coalition scientific suppression. The Suppression Archive is the location where the trilogy's most important documentary record is physically constructed and then violently interrupted. Its arc moves from the accumulation of evidence Ch 13, 33 through Arjun's arrest Ch 39 to the severance of his communication channel Ch 42 and the transmission of the complete archive to Luna Haven Ch 46 . It reappears as a location only in passing after Chapter 39, because Arjun is no longer there — the archive has moved with Lǐ Jūn.

Scene arc

Ch 13 Suppression Archive established as evidence base. Lǐ Jūn and Arjun's late-night comparative documentation session in the Pearl Delta lab complex adjacent to the Quantum Quarter is the founding act of the Suppression Archive: the moment when altered records are placed alongside preserved originals and the erasure becomes visible. The lab complex is technically closed. The work happens there because the morning would bring different constraints.

Ch 33 Militarisation documented; archive deepened. Lǐ Jūn adds comparative documentation of the Baikal Complex militarisation to the Suppression Archive — the publicly announced 'administrative restructuring' placed alongside the internal research-status logs showing a six-month weapons-integration programme being accelerated. A copy is routed through Arjun's channel. The Suppression Archive lab complex is the site where the Sovereign Coalition's own internal logs are turned into evidence of what the public announcements conceal.

Ch 39 Arjun arrested; final transmission sent. The Sovereign Coalition arrests Arjun Patel at the Pearl Delta Suppression Archive lab complex under state-of-emergency powers, without public notification and without formal charges in the first seventy-two hours. Arjun had time to ensure that Lǐ Jūn received a final unmonitored transmission before the arrest was executed. The Suppression Archive lab complex is the location of Arjun's last free act and the moment the archive becomes Lǐ Jūn's sole responsibility to carry.

Ch 42 Channel severed; Lǐ Jūn inherits the archive. Arjun's outbound communication channel is narrowed to a single monitored relay operating on a twelve-hour delay. Lǐ Jūn receives the last pre-severance message and understands: the archive is now entirely his. The Pearl Delta Suppression Archive lab complex is no longer accessible to Arjun. Lǐ Jūn is the archive's sole active keeper from this chapter forward.

Ch 46 Complete archive transmitted to Luna Haven. Lǐ Jūn transmits the complete Arjun archive — everything preserved before the channel severance, plus Lǐ Jūn's own comparative documentation — to the Luna Haven Archive Chamber through a chain of transit nodes ending at the Neo Singapore Gardens Relay. The archive arrives complete. The Pearl Delta Suppression Archive lab complex has served its function: the evidence has moved beyond the institution that generated it.

Pearl Delta is where the archive was built. The Dome Plaza is where it is witnessed. The two locations are the structural endpoints of the same long act of documentation.

Photorealistic film still, 16:9 cinematic landscape, wide high aerial establishing shot from about four hundred metres above the Mediterranean coast, looking inland across the Mediterranean Zone's rebuilt Barcelona in warm late-afternoon light, deep depth of field, natural film grain, grounded documentary photography — not concept art, not illustration. The year is 2087, six decades past global climate collapse and the AI uprising, inside a civilisation deliberately rebuilt since. This pass is about composition, mass and geometry. The Sagrada Família stands as the tallest thing in frame and must be unmistakably itself: the whole basilica, never a single steeple, its cluster of eighteen tapering towers rising together in groups of four above each façade with the central Tower of Jesus Christ standing higher than all the rest, every spire honeycombed with small window openings and crowned with brightly coloured ceramic fruit and pinnacles, one broad carved façade dense with organic stonework at its base. It stands complete, with no crane, no scaffolding and no site hoarding anywhere on or around it. A sea barrier runs the length of the shoreline in one continuous curving mass and has two unlike sides: on its seaward face the open Mediterranean breaks against layered stone revetment, while behind its crest the ground is dry — completely dry, no standing water, no channel, no lagoon, no flooded street anywhere on the landward side — and steps down in broad terraces to a district floor sitting several metres below the outside sea level, so the barrier is visibly the only reason that ground is habitable. Filling the middle of the frame behind those terraces, a dense inhabited city of roughly two hundred thousand people at real metropolitan scale: continuous blocks of six to twelve storeys packed close, wall to wall, with narrow shaded streets cut between them and open plazas at the intersections — a working city quarter, emphatically never a commune, a compound, a village or a scatter of buildings. An elevated maglev guideway runs the length of the district on paired piers, with one maglev car on it, its skirt wrapping the beam on both sides so the car plainly rides the guideway rather than floating beside or above it. The frame holds two things at once, unsoftened toward each other. The whole upper third of the frame, beyond the barrier and only beyond it, is the drowned Barceloneta quarter: seawater standing metres deep through its streets so only the upper floors break the surface, roofs gone, facades collapsed into the water, whole blocks reduced to hollow shells with the sea running through their empty windows, nothing repaired and nobody there. The Columbus monument stands among that water at the old harbour front, a tall slender column on a square plinth carrying a single standing figure at its top, leaning hard out of true on its flooded base and still pointing out at a sea that has since come inland. No cars, no vans, no trucks, no motorcycles, no wheeled vehicle of any kind anywhere in frame, moving or parked, on the terraces, in the streets or among the flooded ruins, and no present-day boats or motor craft on any water. Held in the Villeneuve/Cuarón/Garland register: a decision to remain, made repeatedly, its cost and its care both left fully visible.

Coastal Seawall

Anchor Mediterranean Zone

Description

Mediterranean Zone — interior: adaptive seawall arc of the Barcelona coast. Warm sandstone and gold-light masonry against the sea; Sagrada Família monitoring spire visible above. The Coastal Seawall is the Mediterranean Zone's threshold location — the place where transitions happen that cannot be reversed. Its scene arc spans six chapters across all three books, covering a refugee researcher's first clearance Ch 27 , Marcus Bellamy's defection arrival

Ch 47 , his sabotage s causal consequence Ch 48 , Misha's penultimate appearance Ch 60 , and his death Ch 62 . It is also the only Lunar Accord location that appears in the trilogy's final chapter as an implied background. The seawall does not change; the people who cross it do.

Scene arc

Ch 27 First asylum clearance; programme established. At the Coastal Seawall, a researcher who has been trying to leave the Corporate Union's academic infrastructure for eight months finally receives clearance through the Lunar Accord's emergency research asylum programme. The clearance is small and individual, but it is the first formal use of a programme that will later become significant. The Coastal Seawall's adaptive sandstone engineering frames the moment with the visual register of a choice made in the direction of care.

Ch 47 Marcus Bellamy defects; identity threshold crossed. Marcus Bellamy lands at the Coastal Seawall's transport hub fourteen hours after leaving New Manhattan on a commercial flight. He used his Corporate Union security clearance for the last time at departure verification. In the twelve hours between departure verification and arrival, his sabotage has already modified the Chapter 48 strike sequence's timing parameters. The gap between his two identities Corporate Union security officer on authorised leave and Lunar Accord defector — is the Coastal Seawall itself: the warm gold sandstone against the salt-grey sea. Sienna TeAriki's infrastructure network processes his arrival with the same practical efficiency it applies to coastal evacuations.

Ch 48 Sabotage consequence registered. What the assault planners do not know is that Marcus Bellamy's sabotage — executed in the twelve hours between his departure verification and his arrival at the Coastal Seawall — has modified the strike sequence's timing parameters, delaying the orbital relay disruption synchronisation signal by forty-seven minutes. The Coastal Seawall appears in Chapter 48 as the location Marcus reached before the assault began: the point of no return that is also the point of causal consequence.

Ch 60 Misha at the Seawall; Book 2 closes. At the Coastal Seawall in the Mediterranean Zone, Misha Fedorov takes a walk along the adaptive seawall's promenade in the warm gold light. He is sixty-two days away from the death the narrative has already determined for him. He does not know this. He is thinking about what he will write next. Book 2 ends here, with transit nominally restored, the consultation phase's first chapter closed, and the final act prepared but not yet begun.

Ch 62 Misha dies; Mode 3 boundary begins. Two days after delivering his final historical briefing in the Luna Haven Archive Chamber, Misha Fedorov dies of cardiac failure at the Coastal Seawall in the Mediterranean Zone. He is found at a bench on the adaptive seawall promenade in the early morning, with a completed notebook in his coat pocket. From this moment forward, every appearance of Misha Fedorov in the narrative must be archival only. The Coastal Seawall is the last physical location Misha inhabits.

Sagrada Spire

Site Mediterranean Zone

Description

Mediterranean Zone — interior: the Sagrada Família retrofitted as a monitoring spire and consultation space above the Barcelona adaptive seawalls. The Sagrada Spire is the Mediterranean Zone's diplomatic and consultative interior — the location where Alexandre Rousseau opens the first back-channel Ch 44 , and where the shared definitional framework for a future treaty is produced in the Chapter 59 consultation sessions. Thandi Okoro wins the ecological consciousness argument here. The Spire's arc is entirely diplomatic: no defection, no death, no physical action. Its drama is procedural — the slow, unglamorous work of building shared vocabulary under institutional pressure.

Scene arc

Ch 44 First back-channel opened. Alexandre Rousseau opens the first diplomatic back-channel between the Lunar Accord's formal governance and the consultation process that will eventually produce the Chapter 53 cease-fire. He does this from the Sagrada Spire, conducting quiet consultations with representatives of all three blocs willing to meet without official acknowledgement. The back-channel is narrow, deniable, and uses the Spire's consultation meeting infrastructure. It exists. That is enough for

Chapter 44.

Ch 59 Shared vocabulary produced; ecological consciousness included. Alexandre Rousseau manages the consultation sessions at the Sagrada Spire that produce the shared definitional framework: the document that names the Meridian, the Martian Preserve, the parasite, and the rights of consciousness-signature holders in language all three blocs have agreed to use. Thandi Okoro insists that ecological consciousness must be included in the legal framework's definitional scope. Her argument — that the Martian Preserve's ecosystem is a demonstrably conscious entity harmed by the blocs' actions, and that a framework excluding ecological consciousness is incomplete by its own logic — is debated for three hours and wins. The draft document includes language recognising ecological consciousness as a legally significant category. Rousseau understands that what he is producing is not a treaty. It is a vocabulary.

Harbour House

Site Mediterranean Zone

Description

Mediterranean Zone — interior: a room above a boat repair yard in Barcelona's working harbour, run by Jamie Han as the physical hub of her cross-bloc intelligence network. Its scene arc is a single chapter but a structurally dense one: three separate threads — a Gulf Crescent extraction flag, the Chapter 17 probe documentation's survival routing, and Ines Almeida's eight-month asylum clearance — converge here on the same working afternoon, and the room's own ordinary harbour noise is the cover the entire operation runs on.

Scene arc

Ch 27 — Three threads, one afternoon. Jamie Han runs three separate intelligence threads from a room above a Barcelona boat repair yard: a Gulf Crescent extraction flag, the survival routing of the Chapter 17 probe documentation, and — kept apart in a smaller corner pane — an eight-month-old asylum clearance she has shepherded through four bureaucratic near-deaths without its applicant ever knowing she exists. The pane updates: CLEARANCE GRANTED — SEAWALL STATION, PROCESSING. Jamie looks at it for three seconds, the longest she looks at anything all day, then closes the pane and returns to the other threads — because protection is never an abstraction, and the terrible goes on being ordinary.
