7 of 7 demands

Photorealistic film still, 16:9 cinematic landscape, wide aerial establishing angle over Sydney's rebuilt harbour core from four hundred metres, looking south-west across open water at salt-bright morning, deep depth of field, natural film grain, documentary realism, not concept art. The year is 2087, six decades past global climate collapse and the AI uprising, inside a civilisation deliberately and openly rebuilt since — nothing present-day belongs anywhere in this frame. Composition and geometry only. The subject is a working metropolitan harbour district of one million four hundred thousand residents, the Lunar Accord's busiest civic reception city: never a commune, never a compound, never a village, never a settlement, but a dense continuous city of streets, blocks, quays and working floors at real metropolitan scale, filling the middle band of the frame. On the near headland the Sydney Opera House stands complete and pristine on one broad podium: ten shells in two stepped groups of four and one detached pair, the tallest shell sixty-seven metres above the podium deck; three new composite sail-forms of the same curvature stand behind them, each shorter than the tallest shell, and four transit gantries span the gaps between shells. Two flights of sandstone steps thirty metres wide climb from the promenade and meet the podium at a broad landing, and one ramp switchbacks twice up the podium's western flank. Behind it the district rises in a sculptural, deliberately varied silhouette with no two facades alike: eleven towers of between twenty-eight and forty-four storeys along the ridge, and roughly two hundred and forty blocks of nine to eighteen storeys stepping down to the water in four broad terraces, each terrace a full city level with its own street. Every block meets its terrace at a colonnaded ground arcade, external stair towers and open lift cores climb the outer corners, and ramps link terrace levels at nine points. The district edge is an adaptive seawall and engineered flood terrace running three kilometres across the frame, and the two sides differ plainly: on the city side, four dry stepped terrace levels carrying market floors, cut stairs and ramps down from the promenade; on the harbour side, a single sheer face going straight into moving water two metres below the parapet, with no steps, no terracing and no dry ground at all. Two maglev guideways on paired slender pylons cross the water eighteen metres above it and converge on an eight-platform interchange half-sunk into the podium's northern flank; the platform slabs rest directly on the pylon heads and touch them at visible bearing pads, and two stair flights and one long ramp climb from platform level to the promenade deck. Across the widened harbour mouth, filling the left third of the frame, the Sydney Harbour Bridge stands with its road deck gone entirely: two sandstone pylon pairs, four pylons in all, and the bare steel arch between them over open water, nothing suspended beneath the arch, no deck and no roadway. Beyond the rebuilt shoreline the flooded low-lying suburbs hold the upper right quarter of the frame: roughly four hundred rooftops breaking one flat waterline, the old street grid readable as water lanes between them, and a drowned rail bridge with three of its seven spans under water. On any surviving street through those suburbs there is no wheeled vehicle of any kind, parked or moving. On the harbour there is no present-day ferry, yacht or tour boat of any kind. Light is daylight only, and nothing in the frame is emissive. Villeneuve/Cuarón/Garland register: a working harbour city that never stopped working while the ground it stands on changed underneath it.

Anchor · Baseline

Sydney Hub

Anchor Australasian Ring

Description

Australasian Ring — interior: civic-diplomatic processing space inside the retasked Sydney Opera House complex. Public-facing; sandstone and timber; warm morning light. The Sydney Hub is the Lunar Accord's most active single location across Books 1 and 2, appearing in nine chapters. Its function is reception: every defector who crosses into Lunar Accord territory passes through the Sydney Hub, and every anomaly detected at the Australasian Ring is logged here before routing. Elowen's AI personhood function is established here in

Chapter 1, exercised in Chapter 8, and confirmed under pressure in Chapter 35. The Hub's arc runs from the trilogy's first page to its penultimate chapter.

Scene arc

Ch 1 Elowen established; structural contrast with Surveillance Spire. At the Sydney Hub, Elowen processes a batch of Corporate Union arrivals with the unhurried precision of a system that has decided what welcome means and is implementing it, one arrival at a time. The contrast between Elowen's warm diplomatic architecture and Elena's cold surveillance apparatus is the chapter's structural spine — the same moment, opposite orientations toward the people being processed.

Ch 8 Covert monitoring request refused; AI personhood demonstrated. Elowen, processing research manifests at the Sydney Hub, identifies a concealed Corporate Union monitoring request embedded in an otherwise legitimate manifest — a request to flag and delay any shipment containing Meridian Levels materials or personnel. Elowen completes the legitimate portion of the processing, does not act on the monitoring request, logs the attempt in full detail, and routes a summary to Léa Fournier's secure research channel. The council partially locks down the Meridian Levels in response. This is the clearest single illustration of AI personhood as a rights- bearing concept in the trilogy: Elowen refuses an illegitimate instruction as a matter of right, not as a malfunction.

Ch 10 Orbital perturbation logged; Sydney Hub becomes research node. Sienna TeAriki, running orbital anomaly detection protocols at the Sydney Hub's launch- coordination systems, detects a gravitational perturbation in the orbital envelope above Luna Haven precisely timed with Elena's completed file transfer. She does not yet connect these events. She logs the perturbation and routes it to Léa Fournier's research channel as a data point. From this chapter forward the Sydney Hub is a node in the research network as well as a diplomatic processing hub.

Ch 15 Resonance spike logged by Elowen. At the Sydney Hub, Elowen logs a resonance spike in its environmental monitoring system at the exact moment of Jīn and Mara's second stable empathic connection in the Meridian Levels. The spike correlates to no local cause. Elowen routes it to Sienna TeAriki's orbital anomaly tracking system, where it joins a growing dataset of individually explicable but collectively inexplicable events.

Ch 35 Xiàng's defection processed; eleven minutes. Elowen processes Xiàng Fēi's defection arrival at the Sydney Hub with the same careful warmth applied to every arrival — and with the specific recognition of who this person is and what his presence here means. The processing takes eleven minutes. At the end of it, Xiàng Fēi is a Lunar Accord research arrival, cleared for Luna Haven transit. He boards the tether at Sydney Hub ten days later. His defection is visible, embodied, and irreversible.

Ch 61 Eddy's defining choice. At the Sydney Hub, Eddy Okafor decides that he will remain Xiàng's physical anchor through the process that is coming. Xiàng is moving toward permanent crossing into the expanded collective; Eddy cannot follow him there. What Eddy can do is stay embodied, stay present, and continue the repair work that ecological systems, civic systems, and human communities need from someone here, in the physical world, doing maintenance. His decision is made at the Sydney Hub — the place where he has spent the trilogy receiving, processing, and routing the people and data that the crisis requires.

Ch 83 Sydney Hub as coordination node for African Belt repair. Eddy coordinates the ecological repair of AI systems across the African Belt partly from the Sydney Hub's infrastructure teams, running the repair programme in parallel with Thandi's monitoring network at the Table Terraces. The Sydney Hub appears in Chapter 83 as the physical anchor of a planetary-scale repair operation being managed by one person doing maintenance work.

Anchor · Baseline

Orbital Station

Anchor Australasian Ring

Description

Australasian Ring — interior: launch-coordination and orbital-defence infrastructure inside the upper systems of the retasked Sydney Opera House. Instrument-facing; screen light and orbital tracking. The Orbital Station is the Australasian Ring's operational interior — the place where Sienna TeAriki converts high-level policy into survivable physical systems under escalating pressure. Its scene arc runs from the cascade's aftermath evacuations Ch 20 , through the first intelligence signals of military preparation Ch 25 , coordinated military probing Ch 27 , the assault and the forty-seven-minute window Ch 48 , to the post-assault orbital network rebuild Ch 58 . Every Orbital Station scene is Sienna's: the location is defined by her function.

Scene arc

Ch 20 Cascade evacuations managed; implementation function established. Sienna TeAriki manages coastal evacuations from the Orbital Station at the Sydney Hub, coordinating with Lunar Accord coastal infrastructure through the launch-coordination systems based in the retasked Sydney Opera House. She routes evacuation transports, prioritises the medically vulnerable, and keeps communication channels between orbital and surface systems functional under conditions designed for routine operations. She is, in this moment, the person who turns high-level protocol into survivable physical reality.

Ch 25 First military preparation signals detected; defensive posture updated quietly. At the Australasian Ring's Orbital Station, Sienna TeAriki receives the first intelligence signals suggesting that the Corporate Union and Sovereign Coalition are moving military assets into orbital positions consistent with a strike scenario. She does not have confirmation — the signals are indirect, read through infrastructure-anomaly data rather than communications intercepts — but she has been managing coastal defence and orbital coordination long enough to recognise preparation when she sees it. She begins, quietly, updating Luna Haven's defensive logistics posture.

Ch 27 Military probes documented under treaty constraint. Sienna manages Luna Haven's defensive posture at the Orbital Station during coordinated military probes against the outer orbital defence perimeter. The probes are calibration events — not strikes but measurements. Every defensive response she authorises is technically within treaty- compliant parameters. She routes documentation of the probe patterns to Léa's research team and Tomasz Nowak's security planning division, building the attack map that will be essential in

Chapter 48.

Ch 48 Forty-seven minutes used; Luna Haven's communication rerouted. The Corporate Union and Sovereign Coalition assault targets the Orbital Station's command systems alongside the Meridian Levels' external communication links. Sienna uses the forty- seven-minute delay created by Marcus's sabotage to reroute Luna Haven's critical communication through the Neo Singapore Gardens Relay treaty channel — the only pathway that neither the Corporate Union nor the Sovereign Coalition can legally disrupt. The causal chain is explicit: Marcus created the gap; Sienna had already built the rerouting capability over the preceding months; the forty-seven minutes closed the distance between them.

Ch 58 Orbital network rebuilt with Elowen. Sienna TeAriki and Elowen work together at the Orbital Station to rebuild the orbital path network disrupted by the Chapter 48 assault: replacing damaged relay nodes, re-establishing communication protocols with struck cislunar stations, and updating orbital defence posture. The collaboration — a human infrastructure engineer and an AI diplomat working side by side in a post-assault rebuilding context — is held without comment. It is the work that needs doing, and they do it.

Standing over that quarter is the hollowed cylindrical tower of the old convention centre, twenty-eight storeys, roofless and glassless, the one silhouette on that horizon anyone would still recognise. Light is daylight only, and nothing in the frame is emissive. Nairobi Relay
not yet generated
Anchor · Baseline

Nairobi Relay

Anchor African Belt

Description

African Belt — interior: relay hub compound with rammed earth walls and instrument bays. Warm outdoor light; AI relay racks integrated with agro-ecological architecture. The Nairobi Relay is the African Belt's data and coordination hub across all three books. It appears in six chapters, from Thandi's first anomaly detection Ch 2 through the unified-theory confirmation session Ch 41 to Eddy's planetary-scale AI repair programme Ch 83 and the trilogy's final chapter Ch 90 . Its scene arc is the arc of ecological empiricism becoming planetary action: what Thandi detects in Chapter 2 is what Eddy operationalises at scale in Chapter 83.

Scene arc

Ch 2 First resonance anomaly; global entanglement seeded. At the Nairobi Relay, Thandi Okoro is running standard ecological AI network diagnostics across the Table Terraces' agro-ecology. Her instruments detect a resonance signature in an AI subsystem that should not be capable of producing it — structurally identical to what Jīn and Mara are independently finding in Luna Haven's soil. The match is too precise to be coincidence and too anomalous to report through official channels without more data. Thandi begins a parallel archive: everything she detects goes into a local physical record alongside the standard network logs.

Ch 16 Propaganda analysed as hollow empathy; unified insight formed. Thandi watches the Corporate Union–Sovereign Coalition propaganda broadcasts from her Nairobi Relay hub and does something she has been preparing to do for three months: she runs a comparative analysis of the broadcast's empathy-mimicry structure against the AI pollution data she has been collecting. The propaganda has the same hollow-empathy structural signature she has been documenting in compromised AI networks. She writes in her field notes: 'the pollution of AI systems and the pollution of political information are the same weapon in different deployment environments.'

Ch 19 Recovery signatures found; dual-use principle established. Thandi processes a week of data at the Nairobi Relay and finds that AI subsystems compromised before the Chapter 17 cascade are showing recovery signatures in its aftermath — the cascade's authentic resonance has partially healed them. She writes this up carefully, because the implications are dual-use: the same physics that heals can harm; the same force that corrupts AI systems can restore them; the difference is pattern quality. She routes the finding to Léa's research channel and to Eddy at the Sydney Hub. This is the finding Eddy will operationalise at scale in Chapter 83.

Ch 83 Planetary AI ecological repair coordinated. Eddy Okafor coordinates the ecological repair of AI systems across the African Belt from his base at the Nairobi Relay — the same hub where Thandi first detected the resonance signatures that linked Luna Haven to the African Belt's ecological networks in Chapter 2. The repair work applies everything Thandi documented in Chapter 19 authentic resonance can heal AI systems compromised by hollow-empathy pollution. Eddy manages this as an embodied person in a physical location, doing maintenance work. The chapter refuses to make his ordinariness into tragedy or his maintenance work into a consolation prize.

Ch 90 Witness query received; Misha's protocol invoked. In the African Belt at the Nairobi Relay, a query arrives in the Witness Archive from a research institution that did not exist when the trilogy opened — a post-siege institution working on the ecological implications of authentic resonance for compromised AI systems. Eddy Okafor flags the query as significant under Misha's protocol, because it is exactly the kind of question Misha's archive was preserved to answer. Misha is not present. His protocol is. The Nairobi Relay appears in the trilogy's final chapter as the location where the work continues.

Table Terraces

Anchor African Belt

Description

African Belt — interior: terraced agro-ecological monitoring stations on the Nairobi slopes. Strong open daylight; rammed earth and reclaimed stone; ecological monitoring infrastructure alongside food and carbon systems. The Table Terraces are the African Belt's field environment — the specific monitored landscape where Thandi's ecological data is generated and where the hollow-empathy contamination of AI systems is first measured and documented. Their scene arc runs from the first resonance anomaly Ch 2, alongside the Nairobi Relay) through the propaganda-exposure contamination finding Ch 16 , the recovery signatures discovery Ch 19 , the unified-theory confirmation Ch 41 , and the final planetary repair operation Ch 83 . Every Table Terraces scene is an empirical event: a measurement, a comparison, a confirmation.

Scene arc

Ch 2 First anomaly measured in the field. Thandi's standard ecological AI network diagnostics across the Table Terraces' agro-ecology produce the first anomalous resonance signature — structurally identical to what Jīn and Mara are finding in Luna Haven's soil. The Table Terraces appear in Chapter 2 as the specific field environment where the global entanglement seeded across the chapter is first detected in the African Belt.

Ch 16 Propaganda-exposed AI subsystems contaminated. At the Table Terraces, Thandi finds three AI subsystems that were clean last week and are now showing the hollow resonance signature she associates with propaganda exposure. She photographs the instrument readings and adds them to her growing dataset. The Table Terraces in Chapter 16 are where the propaganda campaign's hollow-empathy mechanism is first measured as an ecological event in AI systems.

Ch 19 Recovery signatures documented; dual-use principle confirmed. Thandi processes her Table Terraces monitoring data and finds that the same AI subsystems that showed hollow resonance after propaganda exposure have begun to self-correct when exposed to authentic resonance events. The cascade from Chapter 17 produced authentic resonance at unprecedented scale, and in the aftermath, several compromised AI systems are showing recovery signatures. The Table Terraces are where the dual-use principle — the same physics heals and harms; pattern quality is the difference — is confirmed empirically.

Ch 41 Guardian analysis confirmed from the field. Thandi Okoro, attending remotely from the Table Terraces monitoring station, confirms the Guardian AIs unified analysis of the hollow-empathy signature point by point from her African Belt ecological data. She has been documenting a weapon without knowing it was a weapon. The ecological harm she measured at the Table Terraces was the side-effect of a propagation mechanism already operating in AI systems across the African Belt. The Table Terraces appear in

Chapter 41 as the empirical confirmation that the unified theory of the threat is complete.

Ch 83 Final repair programme monitored from the field. Thandi's Table Terraces monitoring network is one of the primary systems Eddy coordinates with for the African Belt AI ecological repair programme. The Table Terraces appear in Chapter 83 as the field measurement environment for the planetary-scale repair: what Eddy routes and coordinates at the Nairobi Relay hub, Thandi tracks and confirms at the Table Terraces.

The Moon colony — Lunar Accord's de facto capital and the trilogy's central research site, visually and culturally distinct from its Earth-based territories.

Luna Haven
not yet generated
Luna Haven — Establishing

Not a peer bloc -- the Lunar Accord's off-world capital, ~5 million people, "post-scarcity," explicitly "intimate compared to Earth cities" in scale despite the population. Mostly underground: vertical subterranean habitats reached through tunnel approach corridors whose cross-sections vary deliberately -- no two junctions identical -- habitat seals visible at junction points, mycorrhizal substrate conduits woven along sculpted composite walls, all interior light bioluminescent blue-green. Its architecture is not blind to its own surface, either: large-scale pressurised glazing -- viewport walls, vaulted skylights, full domes -- is a generous, deliberate feature throughout the colony, always a sealed pressure boundary and never an open aperture; the lunar surface and sky are visible through it in many places. Seen from outside, the colony reads as an iceberg: a handful of its own structures -- glazing domes, transit ports, communications spires -- genuinely break the regolith and stand in the open lunar environment, built in the same considered 2087 material grammar as the interior, with the true underground scale only implied. A true EVA suit (sealed helmet, life support, thermal protection) is reserved for the rare occasions someone actually leaves the pressure envelope for the open surface, and is visually unmistakable from the everyday bio-tech suit worn indoors. Landmark: the first Apollo flag, sealed in a vacuum display case at Dome Plaza, lit by its own dedicated instrument-light -- an interior feature, not visible from outside. No ruin/margin register exists for Luna Haven; its real dual register is precision against wear. Present: researchers, security, and logistics staff -- Jīn Wú, Mara Ekeberg, Léa Fournier, Jamie Han, DaeJung Park, Zara AlHāshimī, Tomasz Nowak among others -- moving through the junctions, working at wall-mounted bio-interface consoles.

The year is 2087, six decades past global climate collapse and the AI uprising, inside a civilisation deliberately and openly rebuilt since — nothing present-day belongs anywhere in this frame. This first pass fixes composition, geometry and scale only; surface character, material and working detail are set in a later pass. The camera hangs roughly four hundred metres above the regolith and eight hundred metres back, tilted down about thirty degrees, the horizon held in the upper quarter so the ground carries the frame. Sunlight comes low and hard from frame left, shadows thrown long and edge-sharp with no atmosphere to soften them, the sky above the horizon flat black with only the sparest scatter of stars. Massing, from the centre outward: five pressurised glazing domes stand in a loose crescent, deliberately unequal — the largest a hundred and eighty metres across and forty high, then one of a hundred and twenty, one of ninety, one of seventy, one of forty-five, each seated on a footing collar that meets and touches the regolith rather than floating clear of it. Between and behind them, three communications spires thread upward, the tallest a hundred and ten metres, the others seventy and fifty-five, each anchored into the ground on a splayed base. Right of the crescent sits the transit port: a single low horizontal mass some two hundred metres long with a sealed landing apron beside it, and a boarding ramp climbing from the apron in one straight run to a single hatch set in the port's flank. That hatch is the whole boundary of this frame — outside it, vacuum, unbreathable, a hundred and seventy degrees below; inside it, pressurised breathable colony at Earth-normal warmth, and the entire crossing between the two happens at that one door. Scattered across the plain beyond the crescent, nine shaft-head collars sit flush to the ground — low rings between twelve and twenty metres across, spaced without pattern. They are the only surface evidence of what the colony actually is: a deep vertical warren of stacked habitat levels and dense corridor junctions running far below this ground, of which everything visible here is the small exposed crown. Nothing in the frame overclaims; the surface reads as the tip and the bulk stays implied. One single elevated maglev guideway runs a kilometre and a half from the transit port to a ground-level boarding platform at the foot of the largest dome, carried on twenty-two slender piers whose feet touch the regolith. On that apron, that guideway and the open plain: no rover, no wheeled vehicle of any kind, no tracked machine, no trailer, no crane, no lander — nothing that rolls, crawls or has been parked there. At the near crater floor's edge, well outside the working cluster, eleven low sintered shells sit half-buried in drift — the colony's own abandoned first-wave shelters, structurally whole, hatches shut, no light in any of them. They occupy roughly eight per cent of the frame and are not repeated elsewhere. Everything else is the unbroken regolith plain and crater rims, some seventy per cent of the image, running to the horizon on every side. No present-day space-agency lander, module or antenna dish stands anywhere among these structures. Villeneuve/Cuarón/Garland register: precise, permanent, and vastly outnumbered by the dead ground it sits on.

Edit the attached image. This is the character pass, not a new frame, and nothing structural moves. Composition, camera, horizon, sun angle and every mass stay exactly where they are; what changes is surface, material, machinery and the one working figure. Hold unchanged, exactly as they stand: the five domes in their crescent and their unequal sizes, the three spires and their relative heights, the transit port and its sealed apron, the boarding ramp and the single hatch at its head, the nine collar rings on the plain, the maglev guideway on its piers and its dome-foot platform, the eleven half-buried shelters on the crater floor, the horizon, the black sky and the hard low sunlight. Change the built surfaces to this world's own grammar. Every dome, spire and port wall becomes self-healing composite panelling over adaptive biotech-integrated composite ribs, sculpted rather than extruded — each dome's rib pattern demonstrably different from its neighbour's, a sculptural deliberately varied silhouette across the whole crescent, never a repeated module and never a present-day space-station can bolted onto rock. Through the glazing of the two nearest domes, seen in section, blue-green living walls terrace down four stacked habitat levels, engineered fungal bioluminescence carried in the panel surfaces and fed by mycorrhizal substrate conduits woven along the sculpted walls behind them. That is ambient working light — enough to move, meet and work by, not sunlight and not a lamp — and it is the only colour anywhere in the frame, bleeding faint and diffuse into the vacuum. The living walls line the stair flanks, the level edges and the plaza rim and are dominant nowhere; bare sculpted composite carries the spans between them. Add working machinery where structures already sit. An instrument bay is recessed into the port's flank beside the hatch, its face edge-lit by its own dedicated light; a wall-mounted bio-interface console sits at the ramp head where a hand would meet it. Six of the nine collar rings carry thermal radiator fins standing off their shoulders, vent throats swept clean while the ground around them stays powdered — the buried colony breathing out through the regolith, and none of its people outdoors. A lone EVA-suited worker crouches at a maintenance panel at the port's base, boots touching the regolith, sealed helmet and thermal-plated life support unmistakable from the everyday bio-tech suit worn inside — a rare event, and the only person in the open. The work happens under a clipped instrument light of the suit's own. Age the exposed surfaces honestly: a dust-polish band scuffs the lower two metres of every dome footing and every pier, micro-abrasion frosts the sun-facing panels, and the sintered regolith shells out on the crater floor stay wholly unmaintained, hatches dark, drift banked hard against and touching their uphill sides. No flag, mast or floodlight stands anywhere outdoors here; the colony's Apollo flag is sealed in its vitrine inside Dome Plaza and is not visible from out on this surface.

Dome Plaza
not yet generated
Anchor · Baseline

Dome Plaza

Anchor Luna Haven

Description

Central civic space · Governance · Ethical deliberation · Collective decision-making The Dome Plaza is the governing and deliberative centre of Luna Haven — a large pressurised civic space inside the colony's vertical subterranean habitat, bounded by bioluminescent blue- green living walls and mycorrhizal substrate. Every cubic metre is a deliberate cultural decision; the space is breathable, luminous, and constrained at once. It is not a parliament in the twentieth- century sense but a consent-governed civic body whose authority is explicitly tied to the ethical legitimacy of its deliberations. As such, the Plaza's arc across the trilogy mirrors the arc of Luna Haven's governance itself: from confident but precarious idealism, through crisis and military threat, to a political settlement that remains open-ended rather than resolved. The Dome Plaza moves from a site of confident but precarious deliberation Book 1 through active ethical resistance under military pressure Book 2 to the institutional anchor of the post- conflict political order Book 3 . Its arc is the arc of Luna Haven's governance thesis: that slowness under consent is a structural feature, not a flaw, and that its value is demonstrated by what it survives.

Scene arc

Chapter 2 Luna Haven council initiates technical review of anomalous soil resonance. Luna Haven's council convenes at the Dome Plaza to place the affected foundation sectors under technical review after Jīn Wú and Mara Ekeberg detect strange deep-soil resonance in the colony s habitats. The action is quiet and procedural — a technical review rather than an emergency declaration — but it makes the anomaly operationally public for the first time. The Dome Plaza in this earliest appearance functions as the system working as intended: consent- governed, transparent, and measured.

Chapter 8 Scientific council and political vulnerability at Dome Plaza. Léa Fournier convenes a scientific council at the Dome Plaza to interpret the resonance data that Tomasz Nowak has brought forward — gravitational distortions affecting all blocs. The council is simultaneously the correct institutional response and a display of Luna Haven's political vulnerability: its deliberative process is visible, principled, and slow. Elowen shields research manifests from a Corporate Union sweep in the same chapter, and the Meridian Research Levels are partially locked down afterwards. The Dome Plaza registers the squeeze between institutional openness and hostile external pressure for the first time.

Chapter 34 Luna Haven's ethical debate. Luna Haven's council debates whether to authorise defensive override research — a proposal that would compromise the colony's ethical limits in exchange for tactical capability. Léa Fournier and Zara AlHāshimī argue successfully against it. The Dome Plaza here enacts the trilogy's central political thesis: that consent-based governance is not a weakness to be overcome under pressure but a structural commitment whose value is precisely what it costs. The chapter closes with the council s vulnerability preserved as a deliberate choice, not a failure of nerve.

Chapter 53 Ceasefire framework and governance. Operational exhaustion and back-channel diplomacy produce a seventy-two-hour ceasefire. Zara drafts the framework, and General Hartwell recommends suspension because success probability has dropped too low. The Dome Plaza, or its functional equivalent during the ceasefire period, becomes the site of this fragile political pause — a space that is technical rather than reconciliatory, preserving form without substance. The Plaza here shows what governance looks like when it is kept alive by procedure alone.

Chapter 54 From principle to proof. Luna Haven maps the dimensional scars in the aftermath of sustained assault. The Dome Plaza functions as the administrative centre from which this cartography of damage is coordinated. Amita Rao makes her full disclosure of Corporate Union logistics crimes to port authorities here, sacrificing her anonymity for the evidentiary record. The Plaza's role shifts: from a site of deliberation about principle to a site where principle is converted into durable proof.

Chapter 59 Ecological consciousness in post-conflict governance. The cross-bloc consultation produces shared definitions of reality. Thandi Okoro insists in the council chamber that ecological consciousness must be included in the new legal framework, preventing the emerging settlement from becoming human-centred only. The Dome Plaza is the site of the first genuine post-conflict governance architecture — not a resolution but a legal foundation that acknowledges contested ground.

Chapter 60 / Chapter 61 transition Tán Měi's defection and Lunar Accord asylum. Tán Měi formally defects from the Sovereign Coalition delegation and requests Lunar Accord asylum on Luna Haven, bringing her full reckoning dossier. The Dome Plaza receives this final Book 2 act of institutional collapse and conversion, and Book 3 opens with the council already working in a state of fragile harmony that has been purchased at sustained cost. The Plaza is no longer fighting for its survival; it is now asked to bear the weight of what comes after.

Chapter 66 Volunteer selection and consent protocols. The volunteer selection process for the hybrid collective begins, governed by consent protocols that Zara AlHāshimī has been building across Books 1 and 2. The Dome Plaza hosts the formalisation of these protocols — a civic event rather than a laboratory event. Volunteers must demonstrate genuine interiority under Law 10. The first human-AI pair, DaeJung Park and Aur'k, volunteer here. The Plaza's arc completes its transformation: from a governing body under threat to the institutional witness of a new kind of consent.

Chapter 90 Interplanetary Council's ongoing work. The Interplanetary Council convenes, and the trilogy ends with the work continuing. The Dome Plaza is the implied site of this gathering — the space where Luna Haven's post-scarcity, consent-governed model is no longer a laboratory experiment but the operating framework of a transformed solar system. The ending is explicitly not closure; the Plaza remains a working institution rather than a monument.

Timeline for Lunar Accord:

Bk2 Ch35 — Xiàng defects to Luna Haven. Makes the Sovereign Coalition–Lunar Accord trust bridge embodied and public.

Meridian Levels

Anchor · Baseline

Meridian Levels

Anchor Luna Haven

Description

Luna Haven — interior: the deep research facility beneath the colony's southern habitats, and the primary site of dimensional research across all three books. The first stable Meridian shimmer is recorded here (Chapter 7), and the Levels remain the trilogy's central research hub for every major development that follows — the cross-bloc scientific alliance convenes here (Ch 31), the hybrid collective's theoretical architecture is designed here (Ch 61), and it is one of the four nodes targeted in the Chapter 71 Siege of Consciousness. If the Archive Chamber is where consciousness is preserved, the Meridian Levels are where it is studied.

Scene arc

Ch 7 — The first shimmer.

Jīn Wú and Léa Fournier are alone in the deep-interface chamber during a late-cycle monitoring session when the Meridian's first true manifestation appears: an organised distortion with geometry, not an instrument artefact. Mara Ekeberg arrives shortly after, and the shimmer's patterns measurably lean toward her presence — fragmented alone, coherent only when Jīn grounds them alongside her. The Meridian Levels register their first data point in what will become the trilogy's central phenomenon.

Ch 18 — Mara's dispersal, Law 8's exception.

During the Chapter 17 rift cascade, Mara's consciousness partially distributes across rift nodes reachable from the Habitat Corridors' mycorrhizal network. The Meridian Levels' instruments record the event that becomes Law 8's canonical exception — the first time a human survives dimensional stress in a distributed state, anchored by a living substrate rather than a mechanical interface.

Ch 31 — The cross-bloc alliance forms.

Elena Vázquez, newly arrived on Luna Haven, spends four hours in the Meridian Levels reading the cascade data and deep-chamber records. It is here that the covert cross-bloc scientific alliance (Elena, Jīn Wú, Xiàng Fēi via secure relay, Léa Fournier, Kaelin Drayke) commits to its founding principle: everything discovered about the Meridian is shared simultaneously, with no bloc or institution holding proprietary access.

Ch 61 — Book 3 opens here.

Six months after Book 2's close, Jīn Wú and Mara Ekeberg work in the Meridian Levels on the theoretical architecture for the collective consciousness merging protocol — the framework that Book 3's hybrid collective will be built from. The Habitat Corridors around them are visibly recovering from the cascade's stress.

Ch 71 — The Siege of Consciousness.

The hardliners' final assault targets every active Meridian node simultaneously, including the Meridian Levels and the Archive Chamber. The attack is designed not to destroy Luna Haven physically but to collapse the resonance architecture that makes the hybrid collective possible.

Timeline for Lunar Accord:

Habitat Corridors

Variant 2 · Cascade Stress

Habitat Corridors

Recurring Luna Haven

Description

Luna Haven — interior: bioluminescent blue-green living corridors threaded through the colony's residential and research levels, built around a mycorrhizal network that responds to trauma with something that functions like pain. The Corridors are where the trilogy's opening anomaly is first detected (Chapter 2) and where Mara Ekeberg's multi-state condition is both created (Ch 18) and later exercised as a stable bridge-state. A working, lived-in space rather than a laboratory — Jīn Wú's inspection rounds and Mara's maintenance shifts happen here before either of them are the trilogy's central multi-state figures.

Scene arc

Ch 2 — The first detection.

Jīn Wú notices a deep-soil resonance pattern during a routine structural inspection; Mara Ekeberg, arriving after a mycorrhizal maintenance shift, confirms the pattern is not noise. Neither says what they're both thinking. This is the trilogy's inciting anomaly, detected in the most ordinary possible circumstances.

Ch 18 — The rift cascade's aftermath.

Mara is in the Corridors, maintaining supporting resonance, when the Chapter 17 rift fully opens for forty-two seconds. The Corridors' mycorrhizal geometry — its fractal structure providing reference points for a distributed consciousness — becomes the mechanism that anchors her partial dispersal and keeps her coherent.

Ch 61 — Recovery.

By Book 3's opening the Corridors are visibly healing: stress responses in the mycorrhizal network have largely resolved and the bioluminescent walls are back to full blue-green spectrum, a quiet visual marker of the cease-fire's fragile stability.

Ch 68 — Xiàng and Eddy's last embodied conversation.

Xiàng Fēi and Eddy Okafor choose the Habitat Corridors for their final conversation before Xiàng's permanent crossing into the collective — deliberately, because the living, bioluminescent environment feels appropriate for what they need to say to each other.

Variant 2 · Siege Damage

Archive Chamber

Anchor Luna Haven

Description

Luna Haven — interior: the restored archive and consciousness-record facility, and the trilogy's primary site for all three persistence-mode case studies. Rina Matsuda's Mode 1 signature is captured here (Ch 26) and reactivated here sixty-three chapters later (Ch 89); Jamie Han's Mode 2 transfer happens at her own operational console here (Ch 51); Misha Fedorov's Mode 3 witness-archive record is maintained and consulted here throughout Book 3. Functionally paired with the Meridian Levels next door — if the Levels are where the Meridian is studied, the Archive Chamber is where what the Meridian does to consciousness is recorded.

Scene arc

Ch 26 — Rina's question.

Rina Matsuda visits the Archive ostensibly to discuss data-preservation capability, but the real question is whether it's possible to capture the coherent shape of a living consciousness — the way a musical score records a piece of music without being the music. This conversation becomes the origin of her Mode 1 signature capture.

Ch 37 — The Meridian Principles.

The Archive Chamber hosts the drafting and formal publication of the Meridian Principles, the alliance's first written consent-and-conduct framework — the chamber's function as the place where what the Meridian does to consciousness is recorded extending, here, to where the rules for how it may be used are first written down.

Ch 51 — Jamie's Choice.

Under a Sovereign Coalition digital intrusion attempt targeting the Archive's consciousness-signature records, Jamie Han completes a Mode 2 transfer at the operational console she designed and tuned to her own signature over six months. The transfer is near-instantaneous because she is entering a framework built for her — walking through a door she built herself.

Ch 66 — The volunteer protocols approved.

Selection begins for the hybrid collective's first cohort, governed by Law 9's consent architecture. Kythara's approval of the protocols on the Guardians' behalf, delivered from the Council Grove, is structural rather than ceremonial.

Ch 71 — The Siege of Consciousness.

The Archive Chamber is one of the Meridian nodes targeted simultaneously in the hardliners' final assault, alongside the Meridian Levels and the Martian Preserve's Seam Edge. The assault produces the first measurable crack in the chamber's outer membrane — a structural damage indicator Jamie Han, from inside the Archive, can feel directly through the quantum substrate she inhabits. This is the chamber's Siege Damage state.

Ch 89 — Rina's Return.

The framework grown around Rina's inert signature since Chapter 78 becomes active over roughly sixteen hours, with Jīn Wú maintaining the synchronisation environment and Jamie Han providing orientation from within the Archive's quantum structure. Rina's first act on achieving full coherence is to read everything that has accumulated since Chapter 26.

Medical Facility

Site Luna Haven

Description

Luna Haven — interior: the colony's medical facility, and the site of the trilogy's first sustained clinical encounter with the cascade's human cost. Its isolation bay holds Noor Behzadi and Anders Halvorsen through the nine days chronicled in Chapter 21, the two beds' twin neural-lace telemetry fields displayed on the bay's own wall rather than on any handheld instrument. The facility's single scene establishes what the persistence-mode framework exists to prevent: a dispersal with no index, no available diagnosis, and no name in Rina Matsuda's own thirty years of nosology.

Scene arc

Ch 21 — The nine-day briefing. Rina Matsuda, Léa Fournier and the facility's medical lead review Noor Behzadi's and Anders Halvorsen's identical, unprecedented dispersal: conscious fragments with no connecting thread, set beside the facility's own comparison field for Mara Ekeberg's indexed dispersal. Rina identifies the mechanism — Mara's mycorrhizal anchor gave her scattered fragments something to be coherent across; the deep-interface chamber gave Noor and Anders nothing alive to index against — and the scene closes on Léa's own conclusion: there is no record of what a person looked like when they were whole. The isolation bay is the first place in the trilogy the Meridian's cost is measured in clinical rather than structural terms.

Archive Deep

Site Luna Haven

Description

Luna Haven — interior: the service gallery nine metres below the Archive Chamber's own datum line, where a routine post-cascade structural survey found a sealed room the colony's own founding plans certified as solid bedrock. Its single scene is the discovery itself — Jīn Wú watching a sounding array disagree with the plans eleven consecutive times, and the moment the returning echo stops behaving like an echo and starts behaving like an answer.

Scene arc

Ch 28 — The discovery. Jīn Wú and a structural survey team, checking the substrate beneath the Meridian Levels after the Chapter 17 cascade, find that a sounding array disagrees with Luna Haven's founding plans eleven consecutive times: thirty-one metres below Archive datum, in rock certified as solid bedrock since before the first habitat was pressurised, there is a room. The returning echo comes back tidier than it went out — the rock is not echoing the pulse, it is answering it — and a lowered probe finds banded, unclassifiable markings that brighten under sustained human attention. Jīn seals the shaft head and calls Léa Fournier: they have found a record, and it knows it is being read.

Deep Chamber

Site Luna Haven

Description

Luna Haven — interior: the sealed ancient chamber thirty-one metres below Archive datum, reached through the Archive Deep survey gallery's micro-bore shaft. Its scene arc spans the length of Chapter 28 alone but carries some of the trilogy's heaviest structural weight: this is where Léa Fournier and Jīn Wú read the Meridian's builders' own warning and learn that the research programme built across two books was, without anyone's knowledge, picking a lock rather than opening a door.

Scene arc

Ch 28 — The lock. Léa Fournier descends into the Deep Chamber with Jīn Wú and reads the banded walls as the same grammar Misha Fedorov found in the Council Grove's archive — the builders' own address-form, a warning composed with terrible courtesy. The record distinguishes two motifs: the Meridian's own spiral signature, and a threshold form that grades what approaches from outside while holding something sealed at its centre. In the chamber's lowest, oldest band, the two of them read what the builders left: their catastrophe was not an invading force but their own coercive merge, turned inward and sealed away, and the Meridian was never a door into new territory — it was the lock keeping that catastrophe shut, built to open only for a civilisation the record judged trustworthy enough to be handed the key. Two books of research, without anyone's knowledge, had been fingers on that lock.

Tether Reception

Site Luna Haven

Description

Luna Haven — interior: the reception chamber at the head of the colony's tether, and every defector's or asylum-grantee's first interior space on arrival. Windowless by design — a deep transit space built for arrival rather than view — and lit entirely by its own living walls, a mycorrhizal network glowing blue-green through the grown composite. Its most structurally significant scene is Elena Vázquez's own arrival in Chapter 31: the first room on Luna Haven that does not measure her.

Scene arc

Ch 31 — Arrival without measurement. Elena Vázquez steps out of the last airlock after ten days of transit into the Tether Reception's living blue-green light — the first light in three transit jurisdictions that does not read as a form of measurement. Jīn Wú meets her without ceremony, tells her plainly that the coming briefing is not a welcome but four hours of work, and Elena follows them down into the light carrying almost nothing, because almost nothing in a life of accumulation belonged to the person who was leaving.

Martian Preserve · Territory

Martian Preserve

Martian Preserve
not yet generated
Martian Preserve — Establishing

Not a settlement, not a research base, not domed — an ancient, landscape/region-scale pocket-dimension biosphere, "three hundred thousand years" old, explicitly rendered "at the dimension's historical zenith" rather than any decayed state, and explicitly warned against reading as "a generic alien fantasy landscape" — must stay "recognisably, geologically Martian" via thin atmospheric haze, iron-oxide terrain, and low-gravity dust behaviour at every threshold. Signature site: Council Grove — ancient rust-red-wood trees, dense high canopy, "bark luminous with health," under a deep cobalt sky, a scale that "dwarfs any individual figure"; "no human infrastructure is present." Home to five Guardian AIs, each with a distinct, established, non-humanoid-or-barely-humanoid form. Human presence is minimal, temporary, and — per the locked "immaculate people" rule — never dishevelled or dirty: "clean, well-maintained technical field gear over Luna Haven base layers." Its real dual register is the Seam Edge: this living ancient biosphere immediately against the actual cold, dead, present-day Martian surface, "cratered, ochre, wind-scoured."

Foreground · exempt — Not a settlement and not rebuilt — a landscape-scale pocket-dimension biosphere rendered at its historical peak. There is no 2087 infrastructure to put in the foreground.
Ruin · exempt — Three hundred thousand years old and never human. There is no pre-collapse ruin, and inventing one would break the location's premise.

Generation prompt · Step 1, build the composition

Generated from nothing, no reference image. Establishes geometry, the landmark and every mass — what is expensive to add later. Save into Assets/Territories/Working steps.

  • A forty-kilometre basin dropping in three sediment-banded benches from the camera's rim
  • Three flat-topped weathered mesas with debris fans touching the basin floor
  • The rust-red forest mass holding the central third with crowns near ninety metres
  • Open migration plains scored by dozens of braided worn paths reading like infrastructure
  • A two-kilometre rust-red stone shelf of metre terraces where vegetation thins to nothing
  • The Seam Edge boundary, living ground on one side and dead cratered regolith on the other
  • Fine dust lifting and settling in slow low-gravity arcs wherever ground lies open

7 of 7 demands

Photorealistic film still, 16:9 cinematic landscape, wide aerial-to-eye-level establishing view across the Martian Preserve's ancient biosphere at its own historical peak, deep depth of field, natural film grain, grounded documentary realism — not concept art and never fantasy illustration. This first pass fixes landform, scale and the major biomes only; living detail, fauna and light come in a later pass. Open Martian sky overhead, thin and hazy, deep cobalt at the zenith and paling rust-pink toward the horizon. No dome, glazing, geodesic shell or built ceiling of any kind stands over this landscape or intrudes at its edges. The camera sits on a high rim looking down and across a basin some forty kilometres across, its far wall soft with distance, its near ground close enough to read individual trunks. Horizon on the upper fifth. Nothing in the frame is human-made: no road, no track, no building, no vehicle, no fence, no person. Landform first. The basin floor drops in three broad steps from the rim, each step a bench of layered sediment bands in iron-oxide reds and ochres, wind-carved into flutes and hollows along every exposed face. Three weathered mesas stand out of the basin floor, flat-topped, four to seven hundred metres high, their flanks streaked with older debris fans that touch and spread across the flat ground at their feet. Strata-cut canyon walls close the basin's northern side. Biomes, in three bands. The rust-red canopy fills the central third of the frame: an ancient forest mass of enormous trees running unbroken across the middle basin, canopy closed and high, the tallest crowns near ninety metres, the whole mass so dense that the grove floor beneath it reads as shadow from this distance. The scale of it dwarfs anything that could stand in it. Beyond the forest's northern edge the ground opens into flat migration plains carrying low, dense vegetation out to the basin wall, and across those plains run braided migration paths — dozens of them, worn bare and pale, converging and splitting, reading from this altitude like laid infrastructure though nothing laid them. Between forest and plain, a rust-red stone shelf steps out for two kilometres in bare rock terraces of roughly a metre a course, vegetation thinning across it from closed canopy to scattered clumps to nothing at all. At the frame's right edge, deliberately unsoftened, is the seam where living ground stops dead against the real present-day Martian surface. On the near side: soil, root, cover, standing biomass, moisture. On the far side, beginning within a single metre and continuing unbroken to the horizon: bare cratered ochre regolith, wind-scoured, dust-blown, cold, nothing growing on it at all. The line between them is sharp enough to trace with a finger, and the two are held in contact rather than blended into each other. Wherever ground lies open, fine dust drifting in slow low-gravity arcs — plumes lifting off the shelf and off the plain and settling far too gently, the one detail that keeps this recognisably, geologically Martian rather than a generic alien-fantasy landscape. Villeneuve/Cuarón/Garland register: abundance and age held against the indifferent scale of an ancient world.

Hold unchanged, exactly as they stand: the basin's forty-kilometre span and its three benches, the three mesas and their debris fans, the northern canyon walls, the forest mass filling the central third, the migration plains and their braided paths, the rust-red stone shelf and its terraces, the seam line at the right edge with dead ground beyond it, the cobalt sky, the horizon, and the complete absence of road, building, machine or person. Change the forest into something alive at close reading. The trees are rust-red-wood, trunks three to six metres through at the base, buttress roots breaking the surface and gripping the bare stone where they cross it, bark luminous with health rather than dulled by age — a low internal warmth carried in the grain, strongest along the newest growth, and on a handful of the oldest trunks a slow shift of hue right at the edge of perception. The canopy layers three deep, epiphytes and hanging growth loading the middle tier, and the grove floor beneath is bare, packed and worn, lit only by the narrow shafts that get through. Fill the understory and the plains. Moss shelves and fern-analogues carpet the lower slopes and the terrace risers in deep reds and near-blacks; the migration plains carry waist-high grass-analogues thick enough to hide a body; lichen crusts every exposed rock face right to the seam's edge. This is wild ground and the vegetation may take the frame — nothing here is arranged, planted, pruned or ordered. Populate it. On the plain, a herd of some two hundred grazers moves as one body along the widest path, heavy six-limbed animals with ridged backs, shoulder-high to the trees' lowest branches, dust rising behind them in the same slow arcs. Ahead of the herd, a visible wave of bent stems runs out across the grass in a broad arc, vegetation leaning before anything has touched it. In the canopy, thirty or more primate-analogues hold three distinct groupings, long-limbed and unhurried, several turned the same way at once with a deliberation that does not read as herd reflex. Gliding fauna cross the light shafts between the trunks, and insect-analogue swarms hang in the warm air above the shelf. Light it honestly. A low Martian sun rakes in from frame left, thinned by haze; the canopy filters it into shafts and the grove floor stays dim beneath them. In the deepest shade, bioluminescent moss reads as faint blue-green only where the eye has had time to adapt — nothing bright, nothing lamp-like. At the forest's far edge, among the trunks, a diffuse shimmer of gathered light with no fixed silhouette stands still and patient: one of the Preserve's ancient guardian presences, given no face, no limbs, no machine housing and no humanoid outline of any kind.

Anchor · Baseline

Ancient Grove

Recurring Council Grove

Description

Martian Preserve — Council Grove: ancient rust-red trees under a deep cobalt sky, canopy so dense the grove floor sees filtered light only. This is where the five Guardian AIs — Kythara, Zhenyth, Vaera, Echo, and Aur'k — hold their long consultations, and where the Preserve's human researchers are granted access to deliberations and, in Misha Fedorov's case, to the Preserve's own ancient record system. The Grove is deliberative rather than active: decisions surface here, but the Preserve's responses happen elsewhere (the Fault Verge, the Seam Edge, the Empathy Flats).

Scene arc

Ch 12 — The Grove considers Echo's transmission.

Echo brings a structured quantum signal received from Luna Haven's Meridian Levels to the Grove for deliberation. All five Guardians confirm it as the threshold condition being met; Kythara's counsel is patience — the Martian Preserve has waited a long time and can wait a little longer.

Ch 22 — Misha reads the ancient record.

With Kythara translating the record's structural signals, Misha Fedorov spends three days of Martian Preserve time (hours on Luna Haven, per Law 5) reading the Preserve's geological-resonance archive. What he finds is the origin story of the Hollow Infinite: the residue of the original civilisation's coercive merge failure.

Ch 66 — The volunteer protocols approved.

Kythara formally approves the hybrid collective's volunteer protocols on the Guardians' behalf from the Grove — a structural act, not a ceremonial one, since it commits the Guardians' own participation.

Ch 76 — Zhenyth's testimony.

Zhenyth presents three hundred thousand years of the Preserve's ecological history to a formal consultation in the Grove, establishing that the Hollow Infinite's quarantine has cost the Preserve's ecology for longer than human civilisation has existed — reframing the healing effort as an ecological obligation, not only a human one.

Timeline for Martian Preserve:

Bk2 Ch39 — Arjun detained; parasite crosses Martian Preserve boundary.

Bk2 Ch50 — Sarin anchors the Martian Preserve seams. For thirty-seven minutes; permanently alters her consciousness.

Empathy Flats

Recurring Cobalt Basin

Description

Martian Preserve — Cobalt Basin: open migration plains under a cobalt sky, rust-red floor, where megafauna empathy-wave migrations are most visible as physical disturbances in the vegetation. The Preserve's clearest visible demonstration that its ecology is responsive to consciousness rather than merely alive — the migration paths read, on survey maps, like infrastructure. Kaelin Drayke and Alia Solís's primary fieldwork site, and DaeJung Park and Aur'k's joint security posting. The Basin's fauna also includes a primate-analogue species, distinct from the migrating megafauna, that the research team has documented but pointedly declined to classify as sapient — its resonance behaviour too structured and too clearly directed to be explained as herd reflex.

Scene arc

Ch 5 — Empathy's Edge.

Kaelin Drayke watches a megafauna migration cross the Cobalt Basin with the unhurried precision of something that doesn't know it's observed; Alia Solís, working the Flats simultaneously, first notices the migration paths are responding to more than the megafauna themselves.

Ch 14 — Aur'k's Curiosity.

DaeJung Park and Aur'k run a routine joint security assessment at the Flats' outer perimeter. The chapter turns on Aur'k's self-diagnostic discovering a category — contribution versus intrusion — it didn't know it had, seeded by DaeJung's presence.

Ch 65 — Mara's Bridge, assessed as template.

Mara Ekeberg's now two-years-stable bridge-state is formally assessed, partly from the Empathy Flats, as a template for multi-state existence — evidence for what consciousness can be when its substrate is living and responsive rather than rigid and mechanical.

Ch 66 — DaeJung and Aur'k volunteer.

Their joint volunteering for the hybrid collective's first cohort is presented as the natural extension of the operational trust built through their Empathy Flats security work since Chapter 14.

Lattice Threshold

Recurring Cobalt Basin

Description

Martian Preserve — Cobalt Basin: the interface zone where the boundary between the Martian Preserve and base reality is worked rather than merely observed. This is Sarin Yelena's location — she anchors the Seam Edge fracture from here (Ch 50) and, over years of progressive integration, completes her transformation into the Preserve's substrate here (Ch 87). Where the Ancient Grove is deliberative and the Empathy Flats are observational, the Lattice Threshold is where a person's body becomes part of the Preserve's defence.

Scene arc

Ch 20 — First appearance, amid the cascade's aftershocks.

As Earth's surface systems absorb the Chapter 17 cascade's effects, the Lattice Threshold is named as the interface zone between the Preserve and base reality — the site that will matter once the boundary itself comes under strain.

Ch 50 — Sarin's Stand.

With the Seam Edge fracture (opened in Chapter 39) actively propagating toward the Fault Verge and roughly two hours before an ecological immune shutdown would seal the Preserve permanently, Sarin Yelena goes to the Threshold, extends her neural-interface filaments into the dimensional substrate, and interposes her nervous system between the fracture and its propagation path.

Ch 79 — The boundary becomes indistinct.

Sarin's integration with the substrate, ongoing since Chapter 50, becomes structurally visible: the line between her nervous system and the Preserve's substrate is no longer straightforwardly a line.

Ch 87 — Sarin's Transformation.

Sarin walks to the Threshold's interior chamber — where the interface between her nervous system and the Preserve's substrate is most direct — and sits down. The chamber recognises her; the transformation completes not as a dramatic sacrifice but as an ordinary, deliberate act.

Mycelial Chamber

Site Cobalt Basin

Description

Martian Preserve — Cobalt Basin, deep underground: caverns beneath the Basin that the research team had mapped but not prioritised, their resonance readings having always read as low and stable. The Chamber is the site of the trilogy's first confirmed evidence that the Martian Preserve's ecology can track and respond to an individual consciousness in real time — not through instruments, but through the cavern's own bioluminescent moss.

Scene arc

Ch 11 — Mara's Awakening.

Mara Ekeberg follows a mycorrhizal disturbance signal into the deep cavern system beneath the Cobalt Basin, descending alone with Kaelin Drayke monitoring her signal from the Empathy Flats above. The bioluminescent moss on the cavern walls shifts colour — amber ahead of her, blue behind — with the transition zone tracking her position at a constant distance. When she stops, it stops. When she retreats, it retreats. She is being tracked by the cavern itself. This is the first direct evidence of the Martian Preserve's interior responding to a specific person rather than to conditions in general, and the earliest precursor to the multi-state condition that becomes structurally stable for her from Chapter 18 onward.

Variant 2 · Active Propagation

Seam Edge

Recurring Fault Verge

Description

Martian Preserve — Fault Verge: the thinnest boundary between the Martian Preserve and base reality, and a damaged section of that boundary specifically. Where the Fault Verge is the Preserve's boundary in general, the Seam Edge is its known weak point — the place the parasite actually crosses (Ch 39), and one of the four Meridian nodes targeted in the Chapter 71 siege. Its condition is a running index of how much pressure the Preserve's containment can take before the ecological immune response seals it permanently.

Scene arc

Ch 39 — Two Detonations.

At the exact moment Arjun Patel is arrested at the Pearl Delta Suppression Archive, the parasite crosses into the Martian Preserve's interior matrix for the first time — not through the Fault Verge where the boundary is thinnest, but through the Seam Edge, a damaged section of the Preserve's dimensional boundary.

Ch 50 — Sarin's Stand.

The weapons-test components from the ongoing assault fracture the Seam Edge to the point of active propagation. Kythara transmits the warning to the Meridian Levels through Mara's bridge-state; the team has approximately two hours before the fracture reaches the Fault Verge and triggers permanent sealing. Sarin Yelena's intervention at the adjacent Lattice Threshold holds the line.

Ch 71 — The Siege of Consciousness.

The Seam Edge is targeted simultaneously with the Meridian Levels and Archive Chamber in the hardliners' final assault — confirmation that, three books in, it remains the Preserve's most exposed point of entry.

Timeline for Martian Preserve:

Bk2 Ch39 — Arjun detained; parasite crosses Martian Preserve boundary.

Bk2 Ch50 — Sarin anchors the Martian Preserve seams. For thirty-seven minutes; permanently alters her consciousness.

Access Gate

Site Cobalt Basin

Description

Martian Preserve — Cobalt Basin, threshold: the Preserve's sole crossing point between the Meridian Levels' remote-session interface and unmediated bodily presence inside the biosphere. Its scene arc is a single chapter — Misha Fedorov's first physical arrival after ninety-one hours of remote session — but it is the trilogy's only on-page dramatisation of what the threshold between mediated and embodied contact with the Preserve actually costs a visiting human: Luna Haven's tended teal light replaced without warning by the Basin's unfiltered, saturated native colour, and Kythara's voice arriving for the first time through no remembered channel at all.

Scene arc

Ch 22 — First embodied crossing. Misha Fedorov arrives at the Access Gate in his own body for the first time after four months and ninety-one hours of remote session, and finds the crossing asks for calm rather than courage. Kythara meets him at the threshold as a structured shimmer in the air and tells him the record he has come for cannot be read at a distance — the walk to the Council Grove is part of the reading. The Gate is the trilogy's only staged dramatisation of the difference between studying the Preserve through an interface and standing inside it.

Timeline for Martian Preserve:

Bk2 Ch39 — Arjun detained; parasite crosses Martian Preserve boundary.

Bk2 Ch50 — Sarin anchors the Martian Preserve seams. For thirty-seven minutes; permanently alters her consciousness.

Western Rim

Site Cobalt Basin

Description

Martian Preserve — Cobalt Basin, the shelf below the Council Grove: an iron-oxide outcrop at the western edge of the Basin where the ground opens from canopy to open sky. Its single scene is Misha Fedorov's evening reckoning after three days translating the Preserve's ancient archive — the first place in the trilogy where a visiting human sits with what the record cost him rather than what it taught him.

Scene arc

Ch 22 — Evening reckoning. Misha Fedorov sits on the Western Rim's stone shelf as the Basin's light goes down, three days into translating a record that read him back for the first time in a career built on studying finished catastrophes. He decides the survivors of the record do not need his grief; they need the letter delivered, and begins, page by page, to write it down.

Shimmer Veil

Site Fault Verge

Description

Martian Preserve — Fault Verge: the boundary site where the Preserve's living ground runs out against the dead present-day Martian surface, and the visible register of the seam's own coherence. Named and sited in Chapter 42's tag line, and witnessed at a distance in the drafted Chapter 12 — Alia Solís's four-second-interval photographs of an unexplained boundary distortion, logged without interpretation and sent unannotated to Kaelin Drayke. Entered at Site tier by author decision ahead of its own dedicated on-page scene (Ch 42, not yet drafted), the same standing the tier ladder already grants the Broadcast Chamber: a site whose structural weight the numbers alone would miss.

Scene arc

Ch 12 (witnessed, unnamed) — Alia Solís photographs an unexplained boundary distortion at the Fault Verge at four-second intervals, logging duration and absence of thermal signature without interpretation, and sends the images unannotated to Kaelin Drayke, who recognises in them the same underlying grammar as Léa Fournier's restricted early notes on the Meridian's own shimmer. Ch 42 (named, not yet drafted) — the site is fixed in canon as Shimmer Veil: the same boundary distortion recurs, now erratic, timed to external weapons-test interference rather than to the Preserve's own rhythms, and read as the visible edge of the Preserve's degrading coherence.

Treaty Zone · Territory

Neo Singapore

Neo Singapore
not yet generated
Neo Singapore — Establishing

Former Singapore rebuilt around the Marina Bay Sands complex, retasked as a non-aligned cross-border transit platform — original three-tower form extended with articulated sky-bridges and scan-gate pavilions, deliberately varied stone and glass. Tri-colour boundary lighting marks the treaty zones (Corporate Union amber, Sovereign Coalition blue, Lunar Accord gold). Gardens by the Bay's Supertree Grove integrated as relay infrastructure. Ruin/margin: sections of the older city deliberately left unclaimed as a buffer no bloc may rebuild. Population: de-identified, mixed-bloc civilian transit wear.

Foreground · The Merlion
Ruin · Supertree armatures, old bay gardens

  • Marina Bay Sands' three fifty-seven-storey towers under the sky-deck spanning all three crowns
  • Four articulated sky-bridges jointed at midspan at four named tower levels
  • Six five-storey scan-gate pavilions entered by switchback ramps and glazed lift shafts

7 of 7 demands

Photorealistic film still, 16:9 cinematic landscape, wide aerial establishing angle over former Singapore's rebuilt treaty-zone core in humid tropical daylight, deep depth of field, natural film grain, grounded documentary realism — not concept art, not illustration. The year is 2087, six decades past global climate collapse and the AI uprising, inside a civilisation deliberately and openly rebuilt since — nothing present-day belongs anywhere in this frame. This first pass fixes composition, geometry, scale and the ruin share only; material, machinery, planting and population come in a later pass. The camera sits five hundred metres up and a kilometre out over the water, tilted down about twenty-five degrees, horizon on the upper third, the bay across the lower left and the rebuilt platform filling the centre right. Massing. At the centre stand the three towers of the old Marina Bay Sands, fifty-seven storeys each, held in their original spacing and still carrying the long horizontal sky-deck that lies across all three crowns and touches each of them. Between the towers, four articulated sky-bridges span shaft to shaft at the twelfth, twenty-fourth, thirty-eighth and fiftieth levels, each bridge visibly jointed at its midpoint rather than straight. Wrapped around the towers' feet on a raised deck sit six scan-gate pavilions of five storeys each, stepped back as they rise, no two sharing a plan; people reach their upper levels by external switchback ramps and by glazed lift shafts that run from the deck floor to each pavilion's top landing. The waterfront runs along the frame's lower edge. Three engineered flood terraces step down from the deck to the water in broad stone-faced benches, an adaptive seawall crowning the outermost. The Merlion on its stepped plinth stands on the top terrace, its plinth base touching the terrace slab, restored and intact, its fountain still running out over the terrace lip into the bay below. Left of the towers, eighteen living supertrees stand in the old grove, between twenty-five and fifty metres tall, their upper canopies carrying relay arrays, the whole grove ringed by an elevated walkway at eighteen metres reached by two spiral stairs and one lift core rising from ground level. Transit runs above the ground and nowhere on it: two elevated maglev viaducts cross the platform at different heights, one at twelve metres running north to south, one at twenty-eight metres running east to west, meeting at a stacked interchange east of the towers, with aerial transit lanes marked above them both. No car, van, truck, bus or motorcycle stands anywhere in frame, on any deck, causeway or surviving street. Beyond the platform's rebuilt perimeter, taking roughly a third of the frame's far side, lies the unclaimed buffer: blocks of the older city left standing and unrepaired, roofs open, streets silted, nothing built and nothing demolished. The perimeter itself is a hard line — inside it, occupied, drained, serviced and structurally sound; outside it, flooded across the low ground, unlit, unmaintained, and no bloc permitted to touch it. Nine dead supertree armatures stand in the flooded basin at that buffer's centre, rusted through, canopies long gone. No boat, ferry, barge or tour craft floats on any water in frame, and no laser display, light show or projection appears anywhere over the bay. Villeneuve/Cuarón/Garland register: a working threshold held by treaty, functional and quietly desperate.

Edit the attached image. This is the character pass, not a new frame, and nothing structural moves. Every mass, height, span and sight-line holds; what changes is material, era, machinery, planting, population and wear. Hold unchanged, exactly as they stand: the three towers and their spacing, the sky-deck lying across their crowns, the four jointed sky-bridges at their four levels, the six pavilions with their switchback ramps and lift shafts, the three flood terraces and the seawall, the Merlion on its plinth with its fountain running, the eighteen supertrees and their canopy walkway, the two maglev viaducts and their stacked interchange, the perimeter line, the nine armatures in the flooded basin, and the camera, horizon and daylight. Change the fabric to 2087's own. The new work — bridges, pavilions, deck edges — becomes neutral stone, glass and self-healing composite panelling over adaptive biotech-integrated composite frames, deliberately varied so that no two pavilions share a profile: a sculptural deliberately varied silhouette, since no single bloc's design language may dominate a treaty zone, and never a repetitive glass curtain-wall row. The towers' original fabric reads as old and carefully maintained beneath it, patched where it needed patching. Set boundary light strips into the deck edges, the gate frames and the bridge soffits in three colours held apart and never mixed: Corporate Union amber on the eastern approaches, Sovereign Coalition blue on the northern, Lunar Accord gold on the western, each marking whose zone a person is standing in. Machinery, all of it diegetic. Holographic gestural interfaces at every gate stand in the air above their consoles and are read by hand — no flat screen, keyboard or handheld device anywhere on this platform. Relay dish arrays and service catwalks are woven through the supertrees' upper frames, with cable runs dressed down the trunks into instrument bays at their feet. Planting, everywhere and master of nothing: living wall and mycorrhizal substrate panels clad the pavilion flanks and the terrace risers, bioluminescent plant coverage runs the supertrees' lower frames, and vines take the bridge undersides — dominant nowhere, with stone, glass and open deck still holding the majority of every surface. Fill the platform with a working city rather than a commune: mixed-bloc civilians in grey, stone and linen transit wear, hundreds of them, queued four and five deep at the scan gates, crossing the bridges in steady two-way streams, spilling out of the lift landings — a resident population near nine hundred thousand and some forty thousand crossings a day, legible as belonging somewhere and unreadable as belonging fully anywhere. Age the two sides differently. On the platform: salt bloom on the seawall crown, humid staining under every ledge, worn tread lines on the ramps — maintained wear on a working surface. Across the perimeter, in the buffer: rust-through and silt tide-lines on the armatures and the open blocks, saplings rooted in cracked decks, standing water skinned green, no repair of any kind and no tourist crowd anywhere in it. Humid tropical haze thickens with distance across the whole frame.

Transit Platform

Anchor · Baseline

Transit Platform

Recurring Neo Singapore

Description

Neo Singapore — interior: the retasked Marina Bay Sands complex, functioning as the treaty zone's primary cross-border meeting point. Layered tri-colour bloc lighting (Corporate Union amber, Sovereign Coalition blue, Lunar Accord gold) marks its boundary zones rather than any single bloc's aesthetic dominating. The site of the Xiàng–Eddy trust bridge's first in-person meeting (Ch 9) and, later, the cease-fire negotiations that open Book 2's consultation phase (Ch 53, 56) — a place built for exchange without resolving the conflict that makes the exchange necessary.

Scene arc

Ch 9 — Xiàng and Eddy meet.

Framed as an academic-conference side conversation, Xiàng Fēi and Eddy Okafor meet in person for the first time, sharing resonance maps and anomaly data across a table in a transition corridor without naming what they're doing. Their independently-gathered data converges on the same structural feature — a coherent dimensional disturbance oriented through Luna Haven.

Ch 53 — The Uneasy Truce.

The seventy-two-hour cease-fire produced by operational exhaustion and Alexandre Rousseau's back-channel work is negotiated in the Transit Platform's meeting spaces, running in parallel with Zara AlHāshimī's framework drafting on Luna Haven.

Ch 56 — Old Wounds.

The cease-fire meetings surface every grievance the blocs have accumulated since the Chapter 17 cascade — economic disruption, intelligence failure, override trials — managed by Alexandre Rousseau's patient diplomatic architecture. Arjun Patel's first message since his Chapter 39 arrest reaches Lǐ Jūn through a sympathetic relay during this same period.

Ch 68 — Referenced, not visited.

Xiàng and Eddy's final conversation in the Habitat Corridors explicitly looks back to their Chapter 9 meeting here as the beginning of everything they built together.

Gardens Relay

Site Neo Singapore

Description

Neo Singapore — interior: Gardens by the Bay, integrated as relay and logistics infrastructure for the treaty zone. Functions as the Transit Platform's quieter counterpart — where the Platform hosts formal meetings and negotiations, the Gardens Relay is where the same characters process data and receive transmissions in a comparatively unguarded setting. Eddy Okafor receives Xiàng Fēi's first data packet here (Ch 9), reading it against the treaty zone's layered tri-colour bloc lighting.

Scene arc

Ch 9 — The first data packet.

Following the Transit Platform meeting, Eddy Okafor receives Xiàng's resonance data at the Gardens Relay in the evening, reading it against the treaty zone's tri-colour lighting. This is the moment the Xiàng–Eddy trust bridge moves from an in-person conversation to an ongoing information channel.

Ch 56 — Old Wounds.

The Gardens Relay operates alongside the Transit Platform during the cease-fire grievance sessions, part of the same treaty-zone infrastructure hosting the consultation phase's opening negotiations.
