Unregulated Margins · Territory

Turan Waste

Turan Waste
not yet generated
Turan Waste — Establishing

The Turan Depression's dry interior — the Aral basin and the steppe around it, outside every administration since the climate wars emptied the map rather than the ground. The landform is a sea that left: a pale seabed of salt-crusted clay with the old shoreline still legible as a terrace kilometres inland of any water. Habitation is real and unregulated — one substantial self-built town grown outward in rings, and smaller holdings scattered across the plain with worn tracks between them, none of it surveyed, permitted or standardised. Kept by hand rather than by mandate: a Baikonur tracking-dish array the settlements maintain themselves, aligned and clean, listening to orbital traffic they are no part of. Ruin/margin: the Aral ship graveyard, pre-collapse trawlers stranded upright on dry seabed. A maglev corridor crosses without a station. Present: the Kazakh Scar, the corner of this plain that happened to be empty.

Foreground · Baikonur tracking-dish array
Ruin · Aral ship graveyard

  • The old shoreline legible as a terrace kilometres inland of any water

7 of 7 demands

Photorealistic film still, 16:9 cinematic landscape, wide aerial establishing angle from about six hundred metres above the Turan Depression looking west across the dry Aral basin in hard late-morning light, deep depth of field, natural film grain, grounded documentary photography — not concept art, not illustration. The year is 2087, six decades past global climate collapse and the AI uprising, on ground no administration rebuilt and no administration left; nothing present-day belongs anywhere in this frame. Composition and geometry first: the land is a sea that left. A wide pale seabed of salt-crusted clay fills the lower half of the frame, and the old shoreline is still legible as a terrace running the width of the image, kilometres inland of any water, the ground above it darker and the ground below it bleached. On that terrace, and filling the frame's left third, one substantial self-built town: grown outward in rings from a single dense core, roughly nine thousand people, its structures between one and four storeys and no two alike in plan, height or material, with lanes between them that curve because they were walked before they were built. Across the plain beyond it, twenty or so smaller holdings sit scattered without pattern — a walled yard, a stand of trees, a roof — with worn tracks running between them and out to the town, each track a line of exposed clay rather than a made road, and on none of them a car, a van, a truck, a motorcycle or a wheeled vehicle of any kind — no cars, no vans, no trucks, no motorcycles, nothing wheeled, parked or moving, on any track in frame. Standing clear of the town on the terrace's high point, kept and unmistakable, a Baikonur tracking-dish array: four steerable parabolic dishes on lattice mounts, the largest sixty metres across, all four aligned to the same point in the sky, their surfaces clean and their structure straight. The frame holds two things at once, unsoftened toward each other: a place plainly and deliberately lived in, and the scale of a landscape that owes it nothing. The whole right third is the dry seabed proper, and standing upright on it, kilometres from any water, the Aral ship graveyard: eleven pre-collapse steel trawlers stranded upright on the dry seabed, salt-bleached to white, hulls holed through, unmoved and unvisited. On the tracks, in the lanes and on the seabed there is no car, no van, no truck, no motorcycle, no wheeled vehicle of any kind, parked or moving, and no present-day boat, barge or motor craft anywhere on any water. One elevated maglev corridor crosses the plain without a station, entering at one edge of the frame and leaving at the other, carried on piers that touch this ground and serve nothing on it. Held in the Villeneuve/Cuarón/Garland register: freedom and exposure in the same frame, at full landscape scale.

Hold unchanged, exactly as they stand: the camera position and the hard late-morning light; the old shoreline terrace running the width of the frame; the town's rings and every structure's footprint and height; the scattered holdings and the tracks between them; the tracking-dish array at its exact position with all four dishes aligned; the dry seabed's share of the frame and the eleven stranded trawlers on it; and the maglev corridor and its piers. Re-skin every structure in materials used honestly as themselves and never dressed to resemble one another: salvaged sheet steel gone matte with oxide, rammed earth in courses, sawn timber, fired brick, sun-bleached polymer panel, and cannibalised pre-collapse structural steel carried in and reused as beam and post. No two adjacent walls share a material. Make the repair visible everywhere and better than what it replaced — a patched roof laid flatter than the original, a splice neater than the break, a doorway rebuilt square in a wall that is not. Open the ground into hand-made waterworks: gravity channels following the terrace contour from a walled well head, timber sluices at the turns, cisterns beside the larger yards. Plant what water reaches and nothing where it does not: kitchen beds and orchard rows inside the yards, windbreak lines of hardy trees along the tracks and the town's windward edge, and bare salt crust everywhere beyond them, the worked ground and the crust meeting abruptly with nothing landscaped between them. Populate what is already built: several hundred people at ordinary work across the town and the holdings — in the lanes, on roofs, in the yards, at the well head — on foot and nowhere in a vehicle, with penned and pastured animals beside the outer holdings. Keep the dish array by hand: maintenance ladders lashed to two of the mounts, a worn path running to it from the town, and the dishes themselves clean against everything around them. Bound the change: nothing new on the seabed and nothing new in the graveyard — the trawlers stay salt-bleached, holed and unvisited, with no repair, no light and no planting out there. Add no bloc iconography of any kind, no standard-issue fitting, no signage and no flat screen. Add no car, no van, no truck, no motorcycle and no wheeled vehicle of any kind on any track, lane or yard, and nothing that reads as rubble, as a camp, or as provisional.

Kazakh Scar
not yet generated
Anchor · Baseline

Kazakh Scar

Site Turan Waste

Description

Unregulated margins — exterior: a stretch of abandoned steppe in former Kazakh territory, administered by no bloc since the climate wars emptied it. The Chapter 17 cascade's third Earth landfall site, and the only one without a witness: material recovered under Sovereign Coalition floodlights within days, never photographed in situ, because no one lived there to take a photograph. What remains is the scar — vehicle ruts baked into the clay pan, the pale rectangle where the recovery pad stood, a cut fence sagging where no one maintains it. The trilogy's clearest image of the unrebuilt world: a place where something impossible happened and nothing changed, because there was no one left to change.

Scene arc

Ch 17 Landfall, unwitnessed (reference). The cascade's third Earth materialisation arrives in ground no one administers; recovery follows days later, by floodlight.

Ch 23 Script reference. Wáng Sù's package describes material recovered from the Kazakh steppe; in his margin he corrects the imprecise word debris to fragments, because nothing that came through was broken.

Ch 35 Xiàng's stop; first on-page scene. At his defection transport's water-and-power stop in the margins, Xiàng Fēi walks the half-kilometre to the fenced scar and stands there — the physicist meeting, in person and unwatched, the physics he has just refused to weaponise. He photographs nothing, files nothing; the looking travels with him.

Edit the attached Turan Waste establishing shot. This is not the aerial view: it is ground level at one empty corner of that plain, and the attached plate supplies material, palette, landmark identity and light, and none of its camera. Full-bleed 16:9 frame, no letterboxing, no black bars, image fills the entire canvas edge to edge — photorealistic film still — cinematic register: Blade Runner 2049 primary reference, lighting strictly diegetic, no lens flare, no HUD overlays, no schematic annotations — held from a fixed vantage across the fenced scar toward the horizon — a viewpoint that could be kept on any visit, never staged for one discovery. The camera stands out on the clay pan at the fence line, at standing height, a long way from the town and clear of every track; the aperture here is the open plain itself — this is an exterior, with nothing between the camera and the world. From there: the pan running flat to the horizon, seen across at standing height rather than from above; the town's rings and the tracking-dish array reduced to a low line on the far horizon, too distant to read as anything but a line; and the maglev corridor crossing behind it, closer than the town and still not stopping. It is 2087. This corner is the one the Margins did not fill, and the frame is about what is not here rather than what is: vehicle ruts baked hard into the clay pan and holding their shape years after the last of them; the pale rectangle where a floodlit recovery pad once stood, still faintly visible against the surrounding crust; a cut wire fence sagging between leaning posts where nobody maintains it, wind-scoured grass reclaiming its line; and no structure, no planting, no waterworks and nobody's repair anywhere in frame — none of the things this territory is otherwise full of. No bloc iconography of any kind marks the scene, and no marker, plaque or fence sign records what happened here. At most one distant figure stands still at the fence line, a single travel bag beside them, small against the scale of the sky. No car, no van, no truck, no motorcycle and no wheeled vehicle of any kind stands on the pan, on the ruts or beyond them, and nothing has been left behind except the ruts themselves. Hold the register: unglamorised emptiness at monumental scale, and emptiness that means something precisely because the rest of this plain is lived in — a place where something impossible happened and nothing changed, because nobody happened to be standing here.
